DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Apr11 25, 1938.
Reference report of Merch 24,
1938, from Consul Richard F. Boyce,
Yokohama, to the Embassy in Tokyo.
According to information supplied
to Consul Boyce by the manager of the
Ford Motor Company of Japan, Limited,
the Ford Company in August, 1937, had
from 15 to 20 million yen blocked in
Japan. Unable to obtain a permit for
the transfer of these funds tae Com-
pany finally accepted the "informal
suggestion" of the Finance Ministry
and bought one million yen of the Gov-
ernment's "China Incident" bonds.
Since then the Government has been ex-
tremely generous to the Ford Company
and has given it permiseion to trans-
fer to Detroit all of its surplus funds
in Japan. The Company also received
exchange permits to cover remittances
for importe of r materiel at the
rate of 250,000 a month for the next
fifteen months. The Ford Motor Company
refuses henceforth to ship any
material whatever to Japan in advance
of cash remittances to cover payment.
The manager of the Ford Company feels
that his buying of Japanese bonds has
been responsible for the Government'e
generosity and he agrees also thet the
Japanese Government may be trying to
release

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
release American funds in anticipation
of efforts to obtain credit in the
United States. The Fond Company In
material to Japan unlose pormite for
the tranafer of payments hao beon
grentodh
The Singer Sewing Machine Company
has been given permits for the trans-
fer of over three million yen, repre-
senting its 1937 profits.
The Standard Vacuum Oil Company
is regularly given transfer permits
because of Japan's urgent need of cil.
Consul Boyce reports the decided
tendency on the part of firms in the
United States to curtail credit to
Japanese buyers and a tendency on the
part of American firms in Japan to cur-
tail inventories. These developments
are due to fear of further yen devalua-
tion. The Standard Vacuum Oil Company
has reduced ite stock of oil in Japan
from ninety days' supply to 45 days'
supply and has materially shortened its
credit terms in at least one of its
accounts with the Japanese Government.
The Singer Sewing Machine Company has
also reduced its inventories and has
reduced the period allowed for install-
mentpurchases.

Your draft letter to Mr. Sohorr states that the latest
894.51/577
official figures of American investments in China and Japan
are those published in the "Balance of International Pay-
ments of the United States in 1933". On June 20 the Bureau
of Foreign and Domestio Commerce is publishing Economic
Series - No. 1, "American Direct Investments in Foreign Coun-
tries - 1936". This bulletin has on page 16 a table giving
the following figures for American direct investments, end
of 1936:
China

Distribution
7,190,000

The text of the report on page 17 has the following:
1

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.97)
-2
"Investments in China dropped from $114,000,000
in 1929 to $91,000,000 in 1936. The major part of
this decline was due to changes in valuation in cases
where the compiler, in one or both years, was forced
to divide by countries, rather arbitrarily, the in-
vestments of some large companies which reported
totals only. Petroleum and public utilities were the
two industries in which most of the American capital
found employment.
"Amerioan companies in China, registered under
the China Trade Act, had a total capitalization of a
1ittle over $22,000,000. Of that total, less than
$2,500,000 was held in the United States, over
$7,000,000 was held by Americans in China, and the
balance by Chinese and others. Loans by Chinese
banks comprised a fair proportion of that balance.
Only the $2,500,000 was included in the present study,
the $7,000,000 being excluded by reason of the de-
finition adopted in the study, which required that the
owner of the investment should be domiciled in the
United States."
You may wish to consider your draft in connection with
these figures, which may be commented on the press about
the time your letter is received by Mr. Schorr.

Mr. Rentsohler said that, as he had been away from his
office so long, he was not fully informed in regard to the
recent developments but that it was his understanding that
his bank was handling very few credits relating to sales to
Japanese interests of arms or any other commodities of a
definitely military character. He said that he assumed that
Japanese companies preferred to handle such business through
other banks because the National City Bank, having branches
in both Japan and China, might be presumed to be particularly
anxious to protect its interests in both countries. He said
that his bank had extended some credits in connection with
the sale of machine tools which were designed to manufacture
arms and that it had extended one credit to facilitate a sale
of airplane propellers by United Aircraft Exports Corporation
to a Japanese company. He knew of no other credits extended
by the bank to facilitate the sale of commodities of military
character. He said that most of the credits of which he had
spoken to me related to the purchase of wood pulp which,
although it might be used for the manufacture of nitro-
cellulose, was, he believed, in fact being used as usual
for the manufaoture of rayon, and to the purchase of cotton.
Japanese purchases of cotton were, he pointed out, steadily
declining.
As to the nature of the credits, Mr. Rentschler explained
that they were all ordinary short-time commercial credits,
mostly

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
mostly of two kinds - (a) irrevocable letters of credit for
which the Japanese gave cash in advance in New York and under
which the bank made payments to manufacturers in accordance
with the terms of the contracts between them and the Japanese
purchasers, and (b) credits extended to exporters by means of
the purchase by the bank of bills at the time the commodities
are shipped, payment effected within ninety days on the ar-
rival or shortly after the arrival of the goods in Japan.
On the legal point raised in question two, Mr. Rentschler
said that he was not sufficiently informed to give me a satis-
factory answer. He said that it was his understanding that
all arrangements for credits contained a provision stating
that they were subject to the laws of the United States now
in effect or which become effective during the life of the
credit. He said that he would ask Mr. Lancaster, counsel of
the bank, to look into the matter and to call me by telephone
1ater.
May 9, 1938.
Mr. Lancaster called at my office this morning. He
said that he had been asked by Mr. Rentschler to call to
tell me that none of the credits which I had discussed in
my

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.93)
-4
my conversation of April 27 would remain in effect if the
Neutrality Act were proclaimed effective in respect to
Japan.

Memorandum to Mr. Lancaster

Referring to your memorandum of May 2nd regarding
the Neutrality Act: I attach hereto the form of advice customarily
employed by Head Office of the Bank in advising of confirmed
irrevocable straight credits opened by the Bank's branches in
894.5
China and Japan.
You will note the concluding paragraph in red
reading:
1/580
"The engagement and undertaking referred to in this paragraph
being binding only if the shipment(s) proposed shall be
permitted under the American Neutrality Act of August 3l,
i935 as amended, and such proclamations, orders and regula-
tions as may be issued thereunder."
This clause was adopted by the Bank &fter consulting with us.
See our memorandum of September 10, 1937 to Mr. Holman.
Section 3-a of the Neutrality Act, which relates to
financial transactions, provides in part as follows:
"Whenever the President shall have issued a proclamation
under the authority of section l of this Act, it shall
thereafter be unlawful for any person within the United States
to purchase, sell, or exchange bonds, securities, or other
obligations of the government of any belligerent state or of
any state wherein civil strife exists, named in such proclama-
tion, or of any political subdivision of any such state, or
of any person acting for or on behalf of the government of
any such state, or of any faction or asserted government with-
in any such state wherein civil strife exists, or of any
person acting for or on behalf of any faction or asserted
government within any such state wherein civil strife exists,
issued after the date of such proclamation, or to make any
loan or extend any credit to any such government, political
F/WB

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.99)
2
subdivision, faction, asserted government, or person, or to
solicit or receive any contribution for anysuch government,
political subdivision, faction, asserted government, or per-
son: Provided, That if the President shall find that such
action will serve to protect the commercial or other interests
of the United States or its citizens, he may, in his dis-
cretion, and to such extent and under such regulations as
he may prescribe, except from the operation of this section
ordinary commercial credits and short-time obligations in aid
of legal transactions and of a character customarily used in
normal peacetime conmercial transactions."
I am handing to you herewith our file bearing on the matter
of the Neutrality Act.
J.A.Stevenson
JAS:rr
Bnc.
P.S. You will note from the memoranda in the file that we recommend-
ed the inclusion in the Bank's credit advice form of the red ink
clause in view of the provisions of Section 2(b) of the Neutrality Act,
i.e., the so-called "cash and carry" section. This provides for the
filing by the shipper with the Collector of the Fort of a declaration
under oath that there exists in citizens of the United States no right,
title or interest in the articles or material shipped. The negotiation
to the Bank under letters of credit of the shipping documents, we
thought, would operate to transfer an interest in the goods to an
American citizen within the meaning of that Section.
J. A. STEVENSON.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
811.503194
January 10, 1938.
CURRENT AMERICAN LOANS OR INVESTMENT IN
MANCHUKUO AND JAPAN
(1) Investments in Public Securities of Japan and
JAN 20 10
Manchukuo.
There have been no additional investments in the se-
curities of public bodies of Japan or Manchukuo during the
course of this present conflict in the Far East; in fact,
this has been the case since l93l or earlier. The only
exceptions that have come to the Department's attention
894.51/
are recent purchases of Japanese Government securities by
American industrial enterprises having branch plants or
other interests in Japan - notably the purchases by the
58
Ford Company. These purchases were made as part of  ad
2
arrangement to enable the securing of permission from the
Japanese exchange control to remit earnings and dividends
out of the country.
(2) Direct Investments for Industrial Development.
As for new direct investments in industrial develop-
ment in Manchukuo or in Japan, there have been no indications
of suoh investments. The American oil companies, trading
companies, et cetera, which have had installations in Man-
F/FG
chukuo

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2-
chukuo needed in their business operations there may either
have increased or deoreased these investments slightly.
(3) Commercial Credits Connected with the Export-
Import Business.
There is always outstanding a rather substantial vol-
ume of short term tanking creditsused to finance export
trade. These are extended by American banks both through
their home offices and through their branches in Japan.
There is reason to believe that such financing continues,
though the chief banks engaged in these operations have
endeavored in their contracts to safeguard themselves against
the results of the application of the Neutrality Act.
On just general grounds of obeervation, it is probable
that the total of such short term commercial credits has
fallen rather than increased since the outbreak of the Far
Eastern conflict - though the Department's information is
that the Japanese authorities are trying to enlarge them.
The banks know that they face a risk of having these credits
frozen by order of the exchange control forbidding repayment
(as happened in Germany and other countries from 1930 on)
and I should believe that this risk would be sufficient to
stop them from enlarging their outstanding obligations ex-
cept those that may be seoured by the deposit of gold, etc.
Funds belenging to American enterprises operating in
Japan,

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.98)
一 3 -
Japan, such as the Ford Motor Company, are already frozen,
1.e., they cannot be remitted freely to the parent companies.
It may be of interest to note that in reply to a ques-
tion in the British House of Commons December 4, 1937, as
to the policy in respect of allowing Japan to "finance her
war in China" by advances from Great Britain, the Chan-
cellor of the Exchequer referred to a statement made by
the then Prime Minister on June 23, 1936, as stating the
principle which governs the attitude of the British Gov-
ernment. The Prime Minister's statement of June 23, 1936,
in a debate on the question of the removal of sanotions
against Italy, was as follows:
"... The Government have no power to lend money
to Italy, and they have no intention of seeking
such power. Moreover, any proposals which in-
volved the issue of securities in this country on
behalf of Italy would, in the first instance, be
considered by the Foreign Transactions Advisory
Committee, whose recommendations are reviewed by
the Chancellor of the Exchequer. My right hon.
Friend would not be disposed to consent to any
relaxation of the restrictions on the issue of
foreign loans in the case of Italy in the present
circumstances. Applicants for export credits for
Italy as for any other country will be dealt with
by the Export Credits Advisory Committee solely on
commercial grounds with a view to supporting United
Kingdom exporters, but not applications which relate
to munitions."
"As regards private credits to Italy, the Govern-
ment are anxious that these should not be afforded
except to the limited extent they may be required
for purely commeroial purposes."

Re: Oliver D. Hovey, Dec'd
#Le:894.51/560
Dear Sir:-
I beg to acknowledge the receipt of your
letter of March l4th, in reply to my communication of Feb-
ruary 24th.
5
I havenot troubled you any further 1n the
matter since, because we have been engaged in negotiations
with the Yokohama Specle Bank, 1ts local attorneys, the American
Surety Co. and its attorneys, and in making investigations at
Springfield, Mass., and elsewhere, but the matter has reached
such an impasse, that I am forced to take the matter up again
with you anew.
I must respectfully take issue with you regarding
your statement to the effect that "the situation disclosed***
does not appear to afford any proper basis for action by this
department."
The controversy 1s not one exclusively between
the administrator c.t.a. represented by me and the Yokohama
Specle Bank, Ltd., and the American Surety Company. On the con-
trary, the Imperial Japanese Government 1s involved in the con-
troversy. The bonds involved represent an obligation of that
Government. The bonds are not the obligation of the Yokohama
Specia Bank, Ltd. It takes the position that it only is the
fisoal agent for the Japanese Government, having charge of the
payment of coupons attached to the bonds, and of the bonds them-
B
selves at maturity, and as such agent is bound entirely by the
instructions and authority of the Government in Japan, having no
disoretion to act other than under such instructions and authority.
I take the position that I am correct in my conten-
tion put forward in my letter of February 24th, to the effect
that the Department of State ought to make an effort to assist
4

=== Page 38 === (confidence 0.99)
Seoretary of State
Page #2
Aug. 10/38
the administrator c.t.a., a citizen of the United States, who is
involved in a claim against a foreign Government, namely the
Imperial Japanese Government.
I must also take exception to the portion of your
letter whioh states - "**you therefore doubtless appreciate
that if the parties conoerned are unable to reach an amicable
adjustment, a final authoritative determination of the controversy
would apparently require 1ts submission to the appropriate Judicial
Tribunal."
I need not inform you that the Imperial Japanese Govern-
ment cannot be made a party to any proceedings before any Judicial
Tribunal, and that no court, State or Federal, would have any jur-
isdiction of any suit brought by the ancillary administrator,c.t.a.
against said Government.
I believe, as I said in my letter of February 24th, that
this 1s a s1tuation which requires me to enlist your aid and co-
operation towards approaching two channels, namely, the Japanese
Ambassador in Washington and the Imperial Japanese Consulate 1n New
York. Which can be of help in the matter 1e something that I be-
l1eve you are in a better position to answer than I am. Furthermore
1t may be that this matter ought to be presented through the American
Ambassador in Tokyo, to the proper authorities connected with the
Japanese Government.
Certainly, I will be very glad at any time to present
further efforts and affidavits, if necessary, to assist in disposing
of the matter, but I feel that the representative of this estate 1s
entitled to consideration and justice at the hands of the Imperial
Japanese Government. Certainly, that Government should not shirk
1ts obligation, now long past due, particularly as long as the ad-
ministrator of this estate is wiliing to supply the bond of indemnity
of a representative surety oompany to guard the Imperial Japanese
Government and/or Yokohama Specie Bank, Ltd. from any claims that
may be made subsequent to the payment of this claim by it or them
to the administrator of this estate.
Arbitrarily, as I have pointed out heretofore in my
previous letter, the Yokohama Specie Bank has 1ndicated 1t will ac-
Gept a bond of the American Surety Company only. Just as arbitrarily
the American Surety Co. in spite of offers made by the administrator
to furnish re-insurance or re-indemnity to it, has refused to exeoute
its bond, but the bond of another representative surety company has
been offered and refused.

=== Page 39 === (confidence 0.99)
Seoretary of State
Page #3
Aug. 10/38
Counsel representing the bank now advises me that the
bank has forwarded the matter to the proper authorities in Japan
for instructions and authority to act further in the matter, one
way or another.
In the ordinary course of events, we should
have a definite decision from Tokyo within eight weeks. Now 1s
the proper time, in my opinion, to present the matter through the
proper ohannels in Japan, through the American Ambassador there,
if you believe 1t to be advisable.
Personally, I venture the opinion that it would be
advisable, and I therefore respectfully now present the matter to
you anew, and I urge in behalf of the administrator, c.t.a. that
an effort be made to render to him such assistance that he as a
citizen is entitled to, from his Government, in the presentation
of a claim against a foreign Government.

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 3220 of August 29, 1938
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
An Outline of General Account
Budget for the Fisoal Year 1938-39.
For the purpose of attaining the object of the present
Japanese military operations in China, it is deemed imperative
for Japan to carry on a protracted campaign. Naturally the
Government is eagerly bent upon fulfilling the urgent demand
of the military authorities for money and substances, while
all possible efforts should be put forth to curtail expen-
ditures in other lines of administration and to refrain from
consuming war materials for other purposes. The budget esti-
mates of expenditures for the 1938-39 fiscal year are drawn
up so as to suit the requirements of the time. The appropria-
tions for new undertakings of the Government offices other
than the Army and Navy were nearly struck out except those for
urgent need. In the budget estimates of revenues, the figures
show an increase in certain items, due to unusual activity in
certain sections of commercial and industrial circles under the
extraordinary situation. The deficiency in revenues will be
supplemented by the issue of loans. Generally speaking, the
budget for the 1938-39 fiscal year is drawn up on the basis of the
following lines of administration:
(1). Appropriations for necessary items concerning replenish-
ment of military equipment.
(2) Appropriations for military relief measures and other
arrangements in conneotion with the present Japan-China conflict.
(3)

=== Page 58 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
(3) Appropriations for air defense, for opening up resources
for agricultural products, for the development of export trade,
for liquid fuel, for the training of technical experts, for
civil aviation, etc.
(4) An increase in reserve funds in Central Treasury in order
to provide for extra appropriations outside the budget estimates
in connection with the present Japan-China conflict.
(5) Subsidies to local finances remain the same as in the
previous fiscal year.
(6) Curtailment of expenditures for new and old undertakings.
(7) Curtailment of expenditures for already-fixed undertakings
of the Army and Navy on account of an increase in Special
Accounts of the Two Services in connection with military
operations on land and at sea.
It should be further noted that the revenues of the
Government railways, communications and Special Accounts (ex-
cluding Special Account of the South Sea Islands Administration
Office) will be transferred to Special Accounts relating to
temporary military expenditures and that for this reason the
revenues belonging to Special Accounts will not be transferred
to General Accounts as in ordinary years.
The total amount of the budget estimates of revenues and
expenditures for the l938-39 fiscal year is as follows:
Revenues.

Law No. 21.
The Government will, besides loans to be issued under
other laws, issue loans or make borrowings to the meximum
amount of ¥73,100,000 in order to cover the expenditures belonging
to the General Accounts for the 1938-39 fiscal year.
If it is necessary to cover deficits in the issue prices
of loans to be raised under the foregoing provisions, the
Government will issue loans or make borrowings besides the afore-
mentioned loans or borrowings.
The present law shall be effective from the day of pro-
mulgation.

principle.

=== Page 110 === (confidence 0.98)

That the Government shall, if necessary, order an alteration
of freight rates for passengers and goods.
That the Government shall, if deemed necessary for the benefit

on the above-mentioned routes by paying a suitable amount of
money.
That the Government, u on the occurrence of an extraordinary
incident, shall use aeroplanes, hangars and other equipment, as
well as airmen and other exployees on land. In that event, how-

That if anyone who receives & subsidy should be found to be
a company, the permission of the Governmen t shall be obtained when

amalgemate or dissolve itself.
That subsidies shall be granted in accordance with Govern-
ment orders. However, the amount of subsidies shall be reduced

which do not possess the above-mentioned capability are used.

Grants-in-aid to unds for Revival of
Business of Japanese residents in China
and Indemnificetion for Losses resulting
therefrom.
Regarding those parties or individuals who will advance
loans for the revival of business of Japanese residents in
general in China during a period from April, 1938, to March,
l940, the Government will effect contracts to allow grants-
in-aid within the limits ofr30,000,000 of such loans and to
indemnify losses resulting from these loans under the following
conditions:
(1) Loans shall be granted on the basis of facts mentioned
.below:
(a) That loans shall only be granted for the revival
of business of Japanese residents in China who have sustained
losses or damages on account of the China incidents.
(b) That loans shall be granted on the basis of a
system of annual redemptions within a period of 15 years.
(0)

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
(o) That the interest on loans shall be four percent per
annum. However, during an unredeemed period the interest
shall be fixed at 3-1/2 percent per annum.
(2) That during an unredeemed period a sum of money cor-
responding to 5 rin per annum against loans advanced shall be
granted.
(3) That indemnification for losses shall cover the whole
of losses sustained by the advance of loans.
That losses in the foregoing paragraph and the amount of
losses shall be decided by the Government in accordance with
contracts for indei.nification of losses and other conditions.
(4) That the Government's permission sha 1 be obtained before
loans shall be advanced.
Inuemnification for Losses sustained
by advancing Loans for Revival of Enter-
prises of Japanese subjects in China.
In case monetary organs have sustained losses (the Govern-
ment will fix a standard in order to decide the amount of losses)
by advancing loans with the Government's permission within
the 1imits of ¥22,000,000 altogether for a period from April,
1938, to March, 1941, in order to aid the revival of the spinning
and the mining industries under the management of Japanese
subjects in China who have sustained losses on account of the
China incident, the Government will effect contracts whereby
to indemnify losses within the limits of ¥1l,000,000 altogether,
provided the indemnification shall not exceed five-tenths of
the total amount of loans advanced.

Loans for Revival of Japanese Shipping
Services in connection with China Incident
and Indemnification for Losses.
The Government will allow grants-in-aid under the conditions
mentioned below within the limits of ¥9,200,000 to those parties
which will advance loans to Japanese shipping companies in order
to revive shipping services which have sustained losses or damages
in connection with the China incident. The Gover ment will also
effect contracts for the inde.nification of losses suffered by
the granting of such loans.
(1) That loans shall e advanced in accordance with the fol-
lowing stipulations:
(a). That loans shall only be advanced in order to make good
the losses and damages sustained by ships and equipment
on land and at sea.
(b) That loans shall be advanced in accordance with an annual
redemption system for a period of 15 years. However, it is
possible to arrange an unredeemed period of less than two years
during that interval.
(c) That the rate of interest on loans shall be 3.7 percent
per annum.
(2) That grants-in-aid shall be given at the ratio of one percent
per annum against the amount of loans advanced.
That

=== Page 119 === (confidence 0.99)
- 14 -
That, should the interest rate of funds to be utilized
for loans exceed 3.7 percent per annum, a sum of money covering
an amount in excess shall be granted together with grants-in-
aid in the foregoing paragraph. That,should the interest rate
of funds to be used for loans not reach the level of 3.7 porcent
per annum, a sum of money corresponding to a balence resulting from
such a shortage shall be deducted from grants-in-aid.
That, should funds obtained by the issue of debentures be
used for loans, the interest of funds  entioned in the foregoing
paragraph shall be given those parties who have issued debentures.
(3) That the amount of indemnification for losses shall correspond
to 70/1o0 against the mount of losses sustained by the granting
of loans.
(4) That the permission of the Minister of State in charge
shall first be obtained in advancing loans.
Special Account

Overseas Affairs.
Chosen Governor-General's Office.
Indemnification for Losses sustained by
Accommodation on Immovable Property.
In the Extra Budget for 1938-39 fiscal year, in which the
indemnification against losses sustained by giving accommodation
on immovable property is stipulated and which has obtained the
approval of the Imperial Diet, "the period up to September, 1938",
mentioned therein shall be altered to "the period up to September,
1941."

Washington, D. C.
SEP191938
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Sir:
This Commission has recently been anprised
through its San Francisco Regional Office of the
activities of certain Japanese subjects in violation
of Section 5(a) of the Securities Act of 1933, as
amended, (15 USC 77e(a)) and Section 15(a) of the
Securities Exchange Act of 1934, as amended, (15
894.51/5
USC 78o(a)) in the sale of certain bonds of the
Imperial Jananese Government.
Investigation has revealed that J. Yonemoto
and F. M. Nonaka, Jaoanese subjects residing in San
irancisco, California, were instrumental in organ-
izing a voluntary unincornoratec association known
as the Japanese Heimusha ai of America. The pur-
6
pose of this organization annears to have been to
2
procure from groups of Japanese subjects in
California and Utah donations or subscrintions
to be transmitted to Japan for the aid and assist-
ance of destitute families of Japanese soldiers.
About August 13, 1938, Messrs. Yonemoto and Nonaka
caused the Japanese Heimusha Kai to forward to
each of the local groups certain literature soli-
citing the purchase of 1,000,000 yen face value
Imperial Japanese Government 32% National Treasury
Bonds, issued 1938 and redeemable not later than
October 1, 1955. Under the terms of the solicitation,
payments by individuals are to be made to the local
groups and forwarded to Messrs. Yonemoto and Nonaka
who will deposit such sums with the Yokohama Specie
Bank, Ltd., of San Francisco and obtain a letter of
credit, which letter will be forwarded to the Yokohama
F/A
Specie Bank in Japan with instructions to mail the
bonds directly to the individual purchasers.

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Section 5(a) of the Securities Act and
Section 15(a) of the Securities Exchange Act make
unlawful the use of the mails or instrumentalities
of interstate commerce to sell or deliver after
sale a security and to transact the business of
broker or aealer unless such security and such
broker or dealer is registered with this Commission
in accordance with the provisions of such Acts.
The bonds of the Imperial Jananese Government have
not been so registered and it is the opinion of
this Commission that the use of the mails and
instrumentalities of interstate comnerce to effect
their sale and delivery after sale is in violation
of Section 5(a) oi the Securities Act. The
Japanese Heimusha Kai of America, J. Yonemoto
and F. . Nonaka are not registered as brokers
or dealers and it is the opinion of this Comnission
that their use of the mails and instrumentalities
of interstate commerce in effecting the transactions
heretofore described is in violation of Section 15(a)
of the Securities Exchange Act.
Ordinarily, and in accordance with its
usual policy, this Commission would immediately
cause an action to be instituted in the appropriate
United States District Court against the Japanese
Heimusha Kai of America, J. Yonemoto and F. M. Nonaka,
to enjoin the continuation of such violation of the
Securities Act and the Securities Exchange Act.
Under the circumstances, however, the Commission
deems it advisable to inform your Department of the
foregoing facts prior to the institution of such
action in order to afford to your Department the
opportunity of making any comment which it may
desire with respect thereto.
Inasmuch as the offering made by the
Japanese Heimusha Kai expires by its own terms
September 30, 1938, immediate action on the part
of this Commission is necessary and your prompt
consideration of this matter is respectfully sug-
gested.

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
This comnunication will be delivered
personally by Mr. Clifton A. Woodrum, Jr., attorney
in the Office of the General Counsel, who is fully
familiar with all of the facts in connection with
this matter and who will be glad to discuss the
same with any representative of your Department
that you may care to aesignate.
Very truly yours,
go.C.mathow
George C. Mathews
Acting Chairman

I
Following consultation with Mr. Hamilton, Dr.
Feis, Mr. Haokworth and Mr. Green,and on the basis of
a consensus of opinion, Mr. Hornbeck telephoned to Mr.
George C. Mathews, Aoting Chairman of the Securities
and Exohange Commission, in regard to Mr. Mathews'
letter of September 14, l938, on the subject indicated
SEP 29 1930
FILED
above. Mr. Hornbeok expressed appreciation for the
spirit of cooperation displayed by the Commission in
granting to the Department an opportunity to offer,
before legal action is instituted, comment and sug-
F
/A
gestions in regard to the matter outlined in the com-
munioation under reference. Mr. Hornbeok said that the
oase
4

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.97)
3
case of Messrs. Yonemoto and Nonaka and their activi-
ties in endeavoring to sell in the United States certain
unregistered bonds of the Japanese Government had re-
ceived consideration by appropriate officers of the
Department who were of the opinion that the issuance
of a warning to the parties concerned would in all
probability promptly bring about cessation of their
and
illegal activities;/that if such warning should fail
of that objective or not bring about a retracing of any
steps thus far taken in violation of the law by those
concerned, appropriate legal action should promptly be
instituted. Mr. Hornbeck added that the action suggested
was, of course, predicated on the assumption that the
Commission has discretionary authority which would per-
mit of such a procedure. Mr. Mathews replied to the
effect that the Commission has ample discretionary
authority to permit of the handling of the case as indi-
cated; that he was favorably inolined to the suggested
course of action; that there were, however, others more
thoroughly acquainted with the details of the case than
was he;and that, after consulting Mr. Woodrum, an at-
torney in the office of the Commission's General Counsel,
he would telephone Mr. Hornbeok with a view to arranging
a mutually convenient hour for Mr. Woodrum to call at
the Department for a further disoussion of the case.
A

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
A few moments after the termination of the con-
versation outlined above and without prior arrangement,
Mr. Woodrum called at the Department. As Mr. Hornbeok
was at the moment otherwise engaged, Mr. Woodrum was
received by Mr. Mackay.
II
Mr. Woodrum said that he had called merely to make
known that the suggestion outlined by Mr. Hornbeck to
Mr. Mathews had been approved and that instructions in
accord therewith would be issued promptly to the Com-
mission's representatives on the West Coast. Mr. Wood-
rum added that in view of his and Mr. Mathews' oral
disoussions of the case with offioers of the Department,
no reply would be expeoted by the Commission to its
letter of September 14.
Shortly after Mr. Woodrum had taken leave he tele-
phoned to the effeot that a further disoussion of the
case with Mr. Robert E. Kline, Acting General Counsel
of the Commission, had resulted in a feeling on the part
of that officer that, in addition to the action indi-
cated, the case should also, unless the Department is
of a contrary view, be referred to the Japanese Embassy
in Washington. Mr. Mackay said that he would consult
the appropriate officers of the Department and would

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.99)
OCT 12 1938
Christian Science Monitor
Japan Seeks Loan
For Plane Plant
LOS ANGELES (AP)—Vice Admiral
Kenji Maebara, retired commander
of the Japanese Fleet and former
chief of the Air Force, announced
Oct. 11 that his country wants to
borrow up to $45,000,000 to establish
an aviation corporation in Mukden,
Manchukuo, to manufacture Ameri-
can-type commercial planes.
The project, he said. would be
backed by the Governments of Japan
and Manchuria. Efforts will be made
to obtain the loan on credit by float-
ing stock.
Already the Admiral and his Chief
Engineer, Y. Kitawura, have visited
the Boeing plant at Seattle. Wash,
and the Douglas. North American
and Lockheed factories here. He
plans to leave for the East Oct. 13.
stopping for other inspections at
Wichita, Kan. Detroit. Hartford.
Conn., and at several cities in New
Jersey.
The large sum is needed to cover
capital building outlays, as well as
purchase of tools and equipment and
the contracting of trained engineers
and mechanics. he explained. He said
monetary drain of the Chinese war
has reduced Japan's investment
power.
The Admiral said the planes must
be convertible into wartime equip-
ment. Present plans. however. are
purely commercial, he added.

INTRODUCTION
Emergenoy conditions in Japan have raised the
question of its ability to continue interest and
principal payments on Japanese foreign currency bonds
held abroad. Any consideration of this problem re-
quires an accurate determination of the amount of
foreign exchange which Japan needs annually to meet
interest payments and redemptions on that part of its
foreign funded debt which is still held by foreigners.
The first six parts of this report are designed
for this purpose and contain such information as could
be assembled and correlated concerning Japan's national,
municipal and corporation foreign currency bonds. This
survey indicates that:
1 - Of Japan's total outstanding foreign currency
bonds of Yen 2,998,923,269, (at current ex-
change rates) foreigners still hold Yen
1,588,338,117. The balance has been re-
patriated by Japanese governmental agencies,
financial and cammercial institutions, and
individuals.
2 - Japan's remittances abroad in l939 for
foreign debt service will require approri-
mately Yen 125,678,896 in foreign exchange,
divided as follows:
National, municipal and corporation
interest payments
￥86,973,305
Sinking fund payments ........ ¥ 20,000,000
Redemption of Osaka Issue of
1909
￥18.702.591
Total Estimated 1939 Payments ¥125,675,896
Japan's

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Japan's willingness to pay its foreign funded
obligations is a matter of record, and there are two
factors arising from the present emergency which
strengthen this desire. They are:
1 - Japan's failure to meet interest and
principal payments on its foreign debt
would result in the immediate withdrawal
of a large part of the short-term credits
it is now receiving from foreign shippers.
2 - Default would act as an additional handicap
to Japan's efforts to obtain foreign credits
for the development of "Manchoukuo" and
North China.
As is indicated in Part VII of the report, Japan
can meet its l939 foreign debt obligations, but in
order to do so it must --
1 - not allow munitions imports in an amount
exceeding those being made currently,
2- allow no imports for regular domestic
consumption,
3 - stimulate exports to other than "yen bloc"
countries, to the extent that they will
exceed by 20 percent the rate of exports
during the first seven months of 1938,
4 - and ship at least part of its remaining
gold reserve.
These requisites indicate the tremendous strain
under which the Finance Ministry will be working when
in l939 it finds it necessary to allocate sufficient
foreign exohange to service its loans.
The

PART I
JAPAN'S NATIONAL GOVERNMENT
FOREIGN CURRENCY BONDS
A. Amounts Outstanding and Annual
Interest Charhes
The Ministry of Finance tabulates the national
government external debt in terms of the old gold par
value of the yen. The total as of August 31, 1938,
was listed as Yen 1,290,763,000. Converted into yen
at exchange rates prevailing on that date the total
was Yen 2,079,645,000 (Table I - A). This sum re-
presents 13.67 percent of Japan's total national
funded debt of Yen 15,213,254,000.
Annual interest charges on the national external
debt amount to Yen 1ll,839,000, of which approximately
Yen 55,718,190 must be remitted abroad.
The Consulate has been informed that the Finance
Ministry estimated as of April 1937, that 50.18 per-
cent of Japan's national external bonds were held in
Japan. At this ratio the value of national external
bonds held abroad would be Yen 1,036,079,139.
The same estimate indicated that 29.07 of Japan's
Municipal Government foreign debt were held in Japan.
The application of these percentages to 1938 national
and municipal foreign debt interest payments would in-
dicate payments abroad of Yen 70,159,287. The Finance
Ministry's analysis of Japan's 1936 balance of inter-
national payments, Page 173 of the Financial and
Economic Annual of Japan for 1937, indicates that
remittances abroad for interest payments on govern-
ment and municipal foreign debt amounted to Yen
72,400,000. After allowance is made for sinking
fund

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
fund redemptions between 1936 and 1938, the margin
of difference between the two sets of figures is less
than 2 percent.
B. Dollar Issues
With the exception of one small franc issue, Japan's
national erternal debt is payable in sterling or dollars.
The two dollar issues are the Gold Dollar Bonds of 1924
and the Gold Dollar Bonds of l930. At current exchange
rates the dollar bonds outstanding total Yen 666,179,000
or 186,530,120. Annual interest payments total Yen
40,342,000 or 11,295,760.
Application of the Finance Ministry's estimate of
total holdings abroad to dollar bonds would indicate
that Yen 331,890,000 or $92,929,000 are held by
foreigners, and t hat annual interest payments abroad
amount to Yen 20,098,000 or 5,627,440.
There are also two sterling issues which are
alternatively payable in dollars. The 5 Percent
Sterling Loan of 1907 may be paid in New York at the
current rate of exchange on London. The prospectus
of the 5t Percent Sterling Loan of 1930 contains
the following statement:
"Bonds will be to Bearer and the Coupons attached
thereto will be payable at the option of the holder in
London in Sterling or in New York in U.S. Dollars at
the fixed rate of exchange of $4.8665 per I 1. .....
Drawn or Matured Bonds will similarly be payable in
London or New York, at the option of the holder ...."
At current exchange rates the amount outstanding
of these two sterl ing issues on August 31, 1938, was
Yen 592,37h,000, with annual interest payments totaling
Yon 30,626,000.
C. Distribution

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
C. Distribution of National External Debt
Of the total national external debt at the end of
1936 of Yen 2,278,193,587 (Table I-) 23.42 percent were
held by Japanese financial institutions, including banks,
insurance and trust companies, and 14.96 percent by
governmental agencies. 1l.80 percent of the total were
held by Japanese private investors and municipalities
and local public bodies.
Distribution of external bonds held in Japan, as
compared with the distribution of holdings of internal
bonds at the end of l936, indicates t hat banks and
trust companies have absorbed a higher percentage of
domestic than external bonds. Insurance companies
and private individuals have purchased, on the contrary,
a correspondingly higher percentage of the external
bonds held in Japan.
D. Maturity Dates of National Government
External Bonds
No national external issue falls due in the near
future. The Sterling Loan of 1907, Yen 390,977,000
outstanding at current exchange rates on August 31,
1938, matures in 1947, and in the following year the
South Manchurian Sterling Debentures of 1923 (Yen
68,571,000 outstanding) are redeemable.

PART II
JAPAN'S MUNICIPAL GOVERNMENT
FOREIGN CURRENCY BONDS
A. Amounts Outstanding and
Annual Interest Payments
Japan's municipal governments had outstanding on
March 31, 1936, at 1936 exchange rates,Yen 373,415,721
in sterling and dollar bonds. This sum was about 22
percent of the total municipal funded debt of Yen
1,677,021,000. Annual interest charges on the ex-
ternal debt amount to Yen 20,076,598. The Finance
Ministry has estimated that 71.93 percent or Yen
269,198,928 were held abroad and annual interest re-
mittances abroad on this amount would total Yen
14,441,097 (Table II).
B. Municipal Government Dollar Bonds
Of the eight municipal foreign issues outstanding
in 1936, only the Yokohama Reconstruction Works Loan
of 1926 had been floated in dollars. In March 1936
the total amount of this issue outstanding at 1936
exchange rates was Yen 57,484,1ll, of which Yen
41,348,321, or  11,577,530 would have been held
abroad, if the ratio of repatriated bonds of this
issue was the same as for all municipal bonds. Annual
interest payments on this latter sum amount to Yen
2,480,899 or $ 694,652.
Two of the other issues, the Tokyo Loan for
Electric Enterprises of 19l2, and the Tokyo Recon-
struction Works Loan of l926, were floated partially
in New York. Yon 255,946,920 of the two issues were
outstanding

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.97)
- 11 -
outstanding at the end of l936. Annual interest pay-
ments on this sum total Yen 13,583,656.
Of issues now outstanding, only two mature prior
to 1950. The Osaka Loan for Electric Railways and
Water-Works falls due in l939 and the Nagoya Water-
Works Loan is payable by l943. The total amount out-
standing in 1936 of these two issues was Yen 35,303,845.

PART III
JAPAN'S CORPORATION
DOLLAR AND STERLING BONDS
A. Amounts Outstanding and Annual
Interest Payments
Japan has outstanding twelve issues of corporation
dollar and sterling bonds, all but two of which were
issued by electric power companies. With the excep-
tion of the Taiwan Electric Power Bonds of l93l, all
corporation bonds were floated between 1923 and 1928.
Japanese Corporation Foreign Currency Bonds out-
standing at the end of 1936 amounted to $142,335,500
and L 3,913,266, or a total of Yen 558,727,336 at
1936 erchange rates (Table III).
According to the Finance Ministry 52.90 percent
of the total outstanding in April l937, were held
abroad. On this basis, Yen 18,21l,456 of a total of
Yen 35,426,192 in annual interest payments would have
to be remitted abroad.
Some of the outstanding repatriated bonds are
owned by the issuing corporations who repurchased
when their bonds on the New York market sank to low
levels in 1930 and 1931. This practice ceased with
the enactment of the Foreign Exchange Control Law in
1933. According to Harold Bell, Taylor, Bird and
Company, Chartered Accountants, representatives in
Japan for foreign holders of Japanese corporation
bonds, the Tokyo Electric Light Company holds
$11,070,000 of a total of $56,701,000 outstanding
from its 1928 issue. Of the I 3,754,450 outstanding
of its 1928 sterling issue, the Company holds L194,100.

PART IV
OBAKA BONDS MATURING IN 1939
The only Japanese foreign currency issue which
matures in the near future is the Osaka Electric
Railway and Water-Works Loan, payable in 1939. This
issue was floated in London in l909 for the purposes
montioned in the title.
The original loan at the old rate of exchange was
in the sum of Yen 30,220,000, of which Yen 12,308,000
had been redeemed by March l936. The balance converted
at the current exchange rate (one yen = one shilling
two pence) amounts to Yen 31,451,674 (Table IV). With
regular sinking fund purchases since March l936, the
total outstanding in May 1939 will be about Yen
26,001,099. If 71.93 percent of these are held abroad
(the average for all municipal bonds), Japan's inter-
national payments in l939 to redeem the Osaka issue
will amount to approximately Yen 18,702,591.
Newspaper reports indicate that the City of Osaka
is now negotiating with the Japanese National Govern-
ment, concerning the refinancing of this issue in the
domestic market. The greater relative. faith which
foreign investors have in Japan's short-term ability
to meet its obligations, as compared with its long-
term ability, is reflected in the current quotations
of the Osaka bonds on the London market. On August
30, 1938, the Osaka Loan of 1909 was quoted at 78.5,
compared with an average quotation of L 44.72 for
all Japanese sterling bonds.

PART V
ANNUAL SINKING FUND PAYMENTS ON
JAPAN'S FORLIGN CURRENCY BONDS
Japan in 1937 reduced its National Government
external debt by Yen 27,042,132 (Table V-A) at the
current exchange rate of one yen = one shilling two
pence or 28 cents American. These sinking fund pur-
chases were confined to four issues - the dollar and
sterling issues of 1924 and 1930.
Municipal redemptions in 1937 amounted to Yen
20,606,040, and those of corporation bonds to Yen
22,965,032. The total 1937 redemption of all Japanese
foreign currency bonds amounted, at current exchange
rates, to Yen 70,613,204.
It is impossible to determine accurately the
proportion of this total which was redeemed from holders
abroad, because the method of redemption varies with
the provisions of the indentures of individual issues.
The Sterling Loan of 1930 provided for sinking fund
purchases by drawings, or if the bonds were quoted be-
low par, for purchases in the open market. Some of the
corporation bonds were issued with the undertaking to
make redemptions by drawings, and others by open market
purohases. Ittis understood that one electric power
company recently attempted to make its sinking fund
redemptions by using bonds it had purchased in 1930
and l931, but was prowented from doing so by foreign
bondholders, on the grounds that such a procedure was
contrary to the terms of the indenture.
Ir

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.98)
- 19 -
If redemptions from foreign bondholders are made
in the same ratio as held abroad, they would amount
to about Yen 35,000,000 annually. As part of these
purchases can be made in the open market at prices
far below par, it is not believed that Japan's total
international redemption payments amount to more than
Yen 20,000,000 annually.

B. Japan's Foreign Exchange Problem
Japan during the period January 1, 1937, to August
31, 1938, shipped to the United States gold in the sum
of Yen 1,305,552,448. During the same twenty months
period Japan's adverse balance of trade with countries
which provide it with foreign exchange ("yen bloc"
areas excluded), was Yen 1,393,962,810. This large
adverse trade balance was accumulated in spite of the
fact that during the last year of this period Japan's
imports were limited largely to munitions goods and to
commodities for processing and reexport.
One explanation of the differential of Yen
88,540,362, which exists between the adverse trade
balance and gold shipments is that it can be accounted
for by Japan's favorable invisible trade. Japan's
current invisible trade is difficult to determine be-
cause of the lack of statistical data, but a check of
the items listed in the Finance Ministry's schedule
of l936 international payments indicates that invisible
income has been sharply reduced. Shipping services, in
particular, have adversely affected. The sharp decrease
in Japan's imports and exports has reduced shipping
revenue, and the large transferral in recent months of
tramp steamers from foreign waters into the transport
service has also been an adverse factor. Revenue
from tourist trade has declined severely. The local
offices of the Dollar Line and Amerioan Express
Company estimate that tourist travel to Japan is not
in emcess of 15 or 20 percent of the pre - "Incident"
travel. Emigrant remittances have apparently increased,
but

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.99)
- 30 -
but a new adverse invisible trade item has arisen in
those purchases made abroad by the Army and Navy which
are not delivered through any customs port of entry.
A more rational explanation of the fact that
gold shipments are somewhat less than the adverse
trade balance is that, although most of Japan's exports
are on letter of credit, it has been ebtaining on many
of its imports short-term credits of 60 to l20 days.
This difference in credit terms existed in past years
and, although there has been a tendency for foreign
shippers to tighten up credit terms, the combined
efforts of importers and the Finance Ministry have
been successful in maintaining many of Japan's short-
term credit channels. A check of monthly gold ship-
ment statistics in comparison with monthly adverse
balances tends to substantiate this interpretation.
Heavy gold shipments have followed by two or three
months the heaviest adverse trade balances, and,
conversely, small adverse trade balances were followed
in two or three months by smaller gold shipments.
The Finance Ministry announced on July 19, 1938,
that it was shipping abroad Yen 390,000,000, of the
Yen 801,000,000 in gold which it claimed to have
remaining in the specie reserve. The explanation given
of this move was that the funds would be used to
import raw materials to revive the export trade. A
study of foreign trade trends during June, July, and
August, would indicate, however, that gold shipments
would have been necessary in any event to settle
adverse trade balances with other than "yen bloo"*
countries.
Gola
Note (*): "Manchoukuo", Kwantung Province, and North
and Comtral Ónina.

=== Page 225 === (confidence 0.99)
- 31 -
Gold shipments to the United States between July
15, 1938, and August 31, 1938, totaled Yen 161,854,641,
and present foreign trade trends indicate that add-
itional shipments will have to be made during the fall
months. There is no reason to believe that Japan will
enter 1939 with gold supply of more than Yen 500,000,000.
Japan's future favorable invisible trade will be small
and its 1939 imports will have to be financed from ex-
port receipts and from gold shipments from this small
remaining supply or from newly - mined gold.
1 - Japanese export trends.
Japan's exports to countries which pay in foreign
currency amounted during the first seven months of
1938 to only Yen 794,934,000*. At this rate annual
exports to these countries would total Yen 1,362,744,000.
The contraction in Japan's exports is due to a
number of interrelated factors the most important of
which are:
1 - Shortage of raw materials arising from
import restrictions.
2 - Diversion of export goods and productive
facilities to military use.
3 - Higher costs of production.
4 - Business recessions abroad.
5 - Boycott movements in several of Japan's
major markets.
The Commerce and Finance Ministries are attempting
to alleviate the shortage of materials by permitting
additional imports for processing into exports and
there are indioations that business conditions abroad
are
Note (*): Monthly Return of the Foreign Trade of
Japan, July 1938.

=== Page 226 === (confidence 0.99)
-32 -
re improving. The other adverse factors remain, how-
ever, and there is an increased tendency for production
costs to rise, and an apparent deterioration in the
quality of export goods.
The reporting officer has recently interviewed a
number of Yokohama exporters who do business with all of
Japan's major markets. They reported that forward
orders indicated only a slight revival in the export
trade, and that any substantial increase in export
volume would be only temporary if domestic inflationary
price trends continue.
If Japan's exports increase by 20 percent over the
level for the first seven months of 1938, annual ex-
port volume would amount to about Yen 1,635,293,000.
This sum is not, however, a net gain in foreign exchange.
At the rate for the fir st seven months of 1938, Yen
571,886,000 of this total would consist of articles
produced entirely in Japan, including such major items
as raw silk, silk textiles, teas and marine products.
The balance of exports amounting to Yen 1,063,407,000,
consists of articles processed from imported raw or
semi-finished materials. The percentage of the total
value of these exports which is represented by the
cost of the imported materials varies withthe imdividual
dtom. Exporters' estimates range from 30 to 60 percent,
according to the line. The Ministry of Commerce and
Industry has established categories ranging from 20 to
70 peroont.
Taking 40 percont of these exports as the amount
chargeable to the imported raw materials, the net
foreign

=== Page 227 === (confidence 0.98)
- 33 -
oxohange would be Yon 638,044,000. This amount added
.to the Yen 571,886,000 of exports of domestically
producted goods, would total Yen 1,209,930,000. This
is about the sum of foreign exchange which Japan can
reasonably expect to get from its l939 export trade.
2 - Japan's import needs.
Japan's import and exchange restrictions were
actively placed in effect in September l937. Since
that time imports have largely been limited to arma-
ment goods and to raw materials for processing and
reexport.
During the year, September 1937 to August 1938,

C. Japan's National Debt Problem
Japan's national funded debt on August 31, 1938,
amounted to Yen 15,213,254,000, requiring annual interest
payments of Yen 633,138,000 (Table VII - C). During
September and the first part of October, an additional
Yen 800,000,000 of bonds have been issued.
Bond issues during the first year of hostilities
were floated with little apparent difficulty, and sur-
face indications reflected little of the economic stress
which is usually associated with emergency conditions.
A study of economic trends in Japan during the last
year indicates that there are five major factors res-
ponsible for this, which may be listed as follows:
1 - Stocks of materials in Japan were at
abnormally high levels at the beginning
of hostilities. Warehoused stocks in
July 1937 were value at Yen 934,725,000,
compared with Yen 663,289,000 in the same
month of the previous year. Large material
stocks were carried by importers, manufactu-
ers, wholesalers, and retailers, and these
added together represented a much larger
accumulation of materials than is generally
realized.
2 - The shipment of Yen 1,305,522,448 in gold
since January 1937, has not only provided
a steady inflow of materials, but has al so
acted as a major deflationary brake on
inflationary trends.
3 - The
Note (*): Compiled by Japan Warehouse Association,
as published in The Oriental Economist.

=== Page 231 === (confidence 0.99)
- 37 -
3 - The China campaign until May 1938 was con-
ducted on a medium - sized scale and Japan's
monetary and materials vequirements were not
excessive. Government spending during the
first nine months of hostilities proceeded
at a rate much smaller than had been anti-
cipated in emergency appropriations.
4 - Although import restrictions became effective
in September l937, prohibitions against the
use of imported materials for processing and
distritution in Japan, did not become effective
until June 1938. In the interim, consumers
were able to purchase necessities and the
consumers' goods industries operated at a
profit.
5 - Japan's export trade in l937 was exceptionally
good, and exports to countries providing
foreign exchange amounted to Yen 368,459,087
more than in 1936*.
It is apparent that Japan's emergency economy
operated during its first year under a combination of
favorable circumstances which will not be repeated in
the future. The reasons for this are:
1 - Shipments from Japan's diminished gold supply
must proceed at a slower pace than previously.
2 - Materials stocks are lower than a year ago,
and in relation to emergency needs are small.
There are no apparent means by which this
shortage can be remedied.
3 - Produotion
Note (*): Monthly Return of the Foreign Trade of Japan,
issues of December 1936 and 1937.

=== Page 232 === (confidence 0.97)
- 38 -
3 - Produotion for regular consumer use has
necessarily been severely curtailed be-
cause of the impossibility of importing
materials for this purpose. This factor,
combined with restrictions on exports
to "yen bloc" areas and a shrinking ex-
port market, has partially immobilized the
productive facilities of the consumers'
industries, which, until 1937, constituted
the basic foundation of Japan's industrial
and financial organization.
4 - Since May l938, the area of hostilities has
expanded and military monetary and materials
demand has increased. Government bond issues
since that date have been running at approxi-
mately the rate anticipated in budgetary
estimates.
1
: 1
There is reason to believe that the present Yen
400,000,000 monthly rate of bond issues is a minimum.
Capital subscriptions, mainly for armament industries,
totaled Yen 2,000,000,000 in the first six months of
1938. Newspaper reports indicate that departmental
ostimates for the 1939 - 1940 regular budget total
Yen 4,600,000,000, or Yen 1,100,000,000 more than
the regular budget for the 1938 - 1939 fiscal year.
These estimates include expanded demands for military
and naval appropriations, but do not include re-
quirements for the "China Inoident". The War Ministry
has been quoted as indicating that there will be no
reduction in fisoal and materials need for the hosti-
lities in China.
If

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.99)
- 39 -
If government bond issues continue at no more
than the current rate, Japan's national debt at the
end of 1939 wil1 amount to Yen 21,613,254,000. This
amount is almost double the l934 national income, which
has been estimated at Yen 11,247,000,000.*
If future bond issues are floated at the prevailing
interest rate of 3.5 percent, annual interest payments
on Japan's national debt by the end of 1939 will total
Yen 857,138,000. This estimate of annual interest
charges is equal to 85 percent of the revenue received
by the National Government in the 1935 - 1936 fiscal
year form all taxes and customs duties.** These
annual interest charges would also represent 56 percent
of the National Government's total regular tax receipts,
stamp receipts, and receipts from the excess profits
tax, during the emergency fiscal year ending March 31,
1938,*** a period of increased taxes and high business
activity.

D. Conclusions
There are many reasons for Jupan to cortinue its
foreign debt service, and in normal circumstunces they
would be controlling. Japan has taken a ood aeul of
pride in its recore of poptly meutine rorein
finaneiul obligations. fter less, tere are no
important bond maturities ror severel yours, and Jupen'
unnual deht service aoroud will a ount to ne more than
Yen 106,000,000.
Dufault on dsnt service ould rsult in a con-
truction of short-tern credits which, from un iamedlate
stundpoint, would be almost s much on econonic struin,
us would the actual servicing of the loun.
Finally, Ju a still  ones that the pucification
of Chira will ultitel reult in tinareil istunce
from ubroad in the auvele unt urd r Ja arese control
of "Manclukuo" and North China. my possibilitis
which may exist in thie respect :ould disupear if
Japan srould fuil to meet its current finuncial
obligutions abrod.
Japan's foreign exchunge position hus been so
weakened, however, the uctual puymente in 1939 will be
extremely difficult, and vill involve not only sucri-
fices by Japanese consumers, but also some reduction
in the scope of Japan's projected armament program.
As has been indicated in Part VII-B, Japan's imports
of armament goods in the last year were substantially
larger than the net amount of foreign exchange which
it

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.99)
-42 -
it can hope to obtain from its 1939 export trade.
The exact import program which Japan will
adopt to meet this disparity is, of course, a matter
of governmental policies. There are at least two
alternatives. Japan could eliminate all imports for
regular consumer use, ship all newly mined gold, and
make some reduction in armament inports. It could,
alternatively, continue armament imports at last
year's rate, make small imports of indispensable itoms
for regular domestic use, and ship abroad newly-mined
gold and part of its remaining specie reserve. This
last procedure would, however, place Japan in an ex-
tremely vulnerable strategic position, should it be-
come involved in hostilities with any other power.
It is apparent that the benefits to be gained
from continuing the foreign debt service will be
weighed against the sacrifices which will be in-
curred by the withdrawal of Yen 125,000,000 in foreign
exchange from other uses.
The long-term prospects for holders abroad of
Japanese bonds is more obscure. Certain definite
trends can, however, be defined.
Japan's national debt by the end of 1939 will be
so large that it manifestly could not be serviced under
a deflationary economic policy. On the other hand, if
present inflationary trends in Japan should become
uncontrollable, government expenditures would increase
rapidly and the ultimate result would probably be the
wiping out of the national debt by note inflation.
Exchange depreciation which would ordinarily accompany
an uncontrollable inflation, would greatly increase the
governmont

=== Page 237 === (confidence 0.99)
- 43 -
government yen expenditure necessary to meet interest
payments on its dollar and sterting bonds. The facts
available indicate that Japan, operating under a free
or semi-free economy, would not be able to service its
national debt in future years.
The present trend in Japan is, however, towards
a completely totalitarian state. This trend arises,
not only from the desire of the military group for
more power, but also from the fact that a free economy
is economically incompatible with Japan's expansion
program.
Under a controlled economy, continued service
of Japan's foreign debt would be at least theoretically
possible.
The decision by government officials on this point
would be made after due consideration of the possibility
of obtaining foreign credits.

appear to the S.E.C. that there is in the

abeyance, and that, while we appreciate being
kept informed, we would not in this instance
ask that we be consulted before the S.E.C.
proceedi, if it sees fit, to such action;
and having been instructed by Mr. Welles to
do so, I called Mr. Douglas on the telephone
and made a statement to him in accordance with
that suggestion and I added that I was speak-
ing under instruction from the Under Secretary.
Mr. Douglas expressed his appreciation
of our having thus replied to his letter.
He said that their investigator was encounter-

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

DATE:
December 5, 1938
SUBJECT:
Financial Conditions in Japan and China
PARTiciPAnTs: Mr. Hall Patch, Financial Adviser to the British
Embassy in China;
Mr. R. K. Bewley, Financial Adviser to the
British Embassy;
Mr. Livesey, EA;
Mr. Luthringer, 'EA.

1—1408
Mr. nall Fatch described briefly the organization
and functioning of the Federal Reserve Bank of North
China. He stated that the Japanese army in China
formulated and put into effect economic policies in
almost complete disregard of the advice and opinions
of the Japanese civilian officials. Occasionally the
latter intervened successfully at "the eleventh hour
and fifty-ninth minute" by flying from Nanking or
Peiping to Tokyo to protest. In the case of the Federal
Reserve Bank of North China, the advice of economic and
financial experts was ignored; the army simply decided
to establish the Bank without giving any thought as to
who

=== Page 256 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
who would run it or how it should be run and further
decided that by march 10, 1939 Chinese national currency
should be withdrawn from circuletion in orth China and
replaced by notes of the enk. r. nall iatch then de-
scribed the difficulties wnich the .ap: nese had experienced
in attempting to force the notes of the bunk into cireula-
tion and to maintain the Chinese uan, the .nchoukuoan
Yuan end the Yen at parity. he remarks of .r. nall .etch
on tuis sub ject added little to the information received by
the Department in its reports from the field.
In the opinion of .r. Hall ratch, General Doihara of
the Japanese army is so determined to replace Chinese cur-
rency in North China by the notes of the Federal Reserve
Bank that the army will insist on the establishment of an
exchange control if that is necessary to the attainment of
this objective. Exchange control would not be effective
in his opinion so long as trade moves through the foreign
concessions. However, the Japanese are already moving toward
the isolation of the foreign concessions at Tientsin by
building a road around them which would cut the concessions
off from their hinterland. oreover, he stated that the
Japanese are attempting to induce the customs authorities
to move the customs house and administration outside of
the foreign concessions.
lir. Hall Patch

=== Page 257 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
Mr. hall Fatch expressed forcefully the opinion that
the imposition of an effective exchange control in North
China by the Japanese would mean that the foreign trade
of that region would completely pass into Japanese hanus.
Furthermore, he stated that sinee horth China was the
Japanese army's "laboratory" for the whole Chin:se venture
the successful introduction of a Japanese controlled cur-
reney and exchange control would be followed by an exten-
sion of similar measures to central China.
Mr. hall iatch stated in strict confidence that the
British and French had been resisting the introduction
of rederal Reserve bank notes by introducing as much
Chinese national currency into .orth China as possible;
e.g. b: sending Chinese notes to lientsin by gunboats
and using them to pay British and Fronch troops. This
he said, was necessary in part because the troops re uire
currency convertible into foreign exchange whereas the
notes of the ederal Reserve Bank were not so convertible.
Moreover, as long as North China could be supplied ith
Chinese currency fear of guerrillas would compel the
peasantry to use this currency rather than the Federal
Reserve notes.
There

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
There was a general discussion of the use of Japanese
currency in China, both the notes issued ty the Japanese
banks and tnose issued by the army, and of the manner in
which this curreney nad im aired the functioning of the
Japanese system of exchange control. .r. Hall ratch said
that the army had failed to anticipate the difficulties
that had followed fron the issle of a large umount of Yen
currency outside of Japan. the army however had persisted
in this policy in spite of the varnings und protests of
Japenese economic experts and even in the face of the
threat of the British financial advisor to the Governnent
of Japan to resign.
In response to questions by .r. uthrineer, r. Hall
Fatch stated that the J:punese had been unwilling to divorce
the Yen circulation in China from that in Japan because of
the resulting damage to Japanese merchants and the effect
on further attempts by the army to finance its operations
by the issue of Yen notes.
In Mr. Hall Fatch's opinion the army had blundered
seriously in attempting to force Yen notes issued by the
army into circulation since practices of this kind made
it much more difficult to induce the Chinese to cooperate
economically with Japan.
In

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
In response to a question by Lir. Livesey, Mr. Hall
Patch said that Yen acyuired by Japanese exporters to
China were sometimes converted into dollar or sterling
exchange in the shanghai exchange market anu thus permitted
the flight of Japanese capital to urope and the United
States. In the opinion of ir. Hall ratch the low rice
of the Yen in the shenghai exchange market (1s cents to
16 cents) indicated roughly the true value of the Yen in
contr st to the artificial val.; maintained y the Japanese
exchan-e control. Mr. hall Fatch said that the flight of
capital from Japan via the shanghai exchange market was at
first a "trickle"but that it was now developing into a
"stream" which was likely to continue to increase in
volume. In addition he predicted the development of a
large "black" market in apan proper.
r. Livesey asked whether only the smaller Japanese
exporting firms were withdrawing capital in this way or
whether the firms associated with the main Japanese financial
groups were also engaged in these practices. r. hall Patch
refused to name specific companies but implied clearly that
the practice was becoming quite general. There were plenty
of Japanese who would be willing to sell large amounts of
Yen at six pence to get their capital out of Japan into
Western countries.
Lir. Hall Patch

=== Page 260 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
Mr. Hall Patch then said thet he had in mind asking
the State Department whether it would object to his asking
the Treasury Department if it should be willing to
cooperate with the British Government in the exchange of
information in regard to Jepanese caj ital in the United
States and the United hingdom. pucifically ne noted
that the statistics of capital movements published by the

Mr. Livesey said that while he would not undertake
to speak for the Department on this guestion he ersonally
saw no objection to asking the Treasury about it and sug-
gested that Mr. Hall Fatch ask ir. hornbeck his opinion
when he saw him later in the morning. ir. Bewley and
Mr. Livesey, however, pointed out the difficulty of
ascertaining the actual nationality of the owners of
migratory capital in view of the powerful tradition of
"bankers' secrecy" in the Netherlands, Switzerland, and
the

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
United Kingdom, which might serve merely as channels for

in the only obtainable computations of capital movements
into the United states.
ir. dall rutch then ex ressed at some length is views
on the identity of British and American interests in China,
pointing out tint although the united states was not being
treated es harshly as the onited kin aom on seconuary issues,
on fundamental issues there was no difference. The Ja enese

There followed ome eneral di cussion of the financial
