WASHINGTON
SIR:
I have the honor to report thet Minister of
Finance Seihin Ikeda and Governor of the Bank of
Japan Toyotaro Yuki attended the 37th annual Kwansai
hkers Convention at Osaka on December 6, 1938.
nister Ikeda remained in Osaka to attend an
onomic round table conference held on December 7
hder the auspices of officials of Osaka Prefecture.
ankers' Convention, Mr. Ikeda took occasion to
publicize some phases of Government finance which
have been purposely obscured heretofore. Among
these were the exact amount of gold shipments in
F/WB
1937 (given as Yen 860,000,000); the progress of
the gold mobilization campaign (regarded as mildly
disappointing except in Taiwan); and a frank discussion
of
FILED

=== Page 263 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
of the size of the deficit, and of the amount of the
note issue in proportion to the resources of the
co untry.
The general tenor of the address indicated
restrained confidence tempered by some foreboding.
Among the problems dwelt on were maintenance of the
market for Government bonds; securing of ready supply
of funds for capital expansion, especially in small
domestic industries, and for exploitation of resources
on the Asiatic continent; low loan rates; state control
which must be at once more extensive and less burden-
some; necessity for expanding foreign trade outside
of the yen bloc; lowering of prices, particularly
on commodities for export; necessity for facilitating
the transfer of unemployed workmen from restricted
industries to vital occupations where a labor shortage
exists; governmental encouragement of savings and
investment among the people; and lifting of the burden
of agricultural debt.
The Minister touched upon the question of the
invocation of Article ll of the National Mobilization
Act in a vague way which left misgivings among those
who fear application of the latter section of that
Article.
The Minister devoted some attention to the
ordinary budget for the nert fiscal year, but gave
no particulars concerning the extraordinary budget.
He

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
He noted with satisfaction that tax receipts have
increased materially.
The Finance ivinister mede few direct references
to the campaign in China, and avoided any discussion
of the objectives sought to be achieved there.
Mr. Yuki in his address emphasized the importance
of thrift in the national economic scheme. He mentioned
that the increase in postal savings during the past
year to ¥640,000,000 or 2% times the amount of the
previous year, was encouraging. However, on a per
capita basis, it amounted to only ¥70 as compared
with annual per capita savings in Great Britain of
¥649 and in the United States of ¥1556, which, he
stated, was a showing far from ideal. The speaker
voiced assurances that the current note issue of the
Bank of Japan has not reached inflationary proportions.
He predicted complete invocation of Article ll of
the National Mobilization Act, and sounded the only
dictatorial note of the convocation when he intimated
that criticism of Government policies was not desired.
Other Government spokemen had declared that the
control system was not perfect, and that corrections
of inequities were to be made.
At the round table conference of economic leaders
of western Japan which was convened on December 7 with
Mr. Ikeda in attendance, the point of view of local
industrialists

=== Page 265 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
industrialists on several controversial issues was made
clear.
Mr. G. Kobate of the Kansai Federation of Industries
pled for more mature consideration of control policies
before their promulgation, and for clarification of the intent
of control regul&tions. Mr. Kobate stated that while a system
of control was inevitable, it was highly important that the
future objective to be attained by control measures should
be clearly and definitely determined. He stated secondly,
that much was to be desired in the administration of control
measures. To quote him, "It appeers to me that control
measures are being appliea at the present tine with no
preparation whatsoever.
There is need for some basic re-
search in this regard."
Mr. Kobata emphasized that from
the standpoint of long-term reconstruction, there was need
for a highly trained body of government specielists.
Mr. M. Ogura, Director General of the Sumitomo interests,
intimated that domestic business in Jepan is now at or
near the inflationary stage, and requested that steps
be taken to ameliorate effects of the deflation which is
bound to take place eventually by modifying tax policies
to enable corporations to accumulate larger reserves.
Mr. Kataoka of the Osaka Chamber of Commerce and
Industry, and Mr. H. Mori of the House of Peers expressed
an interest in tax reform.
Mr. Kurimoto of the Institute for study of politics
and economics complained that normal credit operations
in

=== Page 266 === (confidence 0.99)
-5 -
in foreign exchange transactions were ampered by
the enforcement of the export-import link system,
and requested that steps be taken to facilitate the
financing of foreign trade. He also advocated
decentralization of control by giving greater discre-
tionary powers in economic matters to prefectural
governors. Mr. Kataoka stated that abuses had been
created by the granting of monopolistic powers to
industrial guilds which discriminated against small
firms not members of the guilds. He recommended
corrective steps in this regard. Mr. Nakayama of the
Osaka Chamber of Commerce and Industry stated that
unemployment could be alleviated by "commodity
mobilization", or the impartial allocation of raw
materials to industrial consumers by the government.
Mr. Nakayama stressed the unemployment problem exist-
ing in the smaller industries and suggested that its
relief depended to a large extent upon solution of
the problem of supply and demand of raw materials.
He complained that commodity mobilization was not
working satisfactorily, blaming this on faulty
administration by the Department of Commerce and
Industry.
Mr. S. Nango of the Nippon Cotton Trading
Company advocated the curbing of industrial activity
in Manchuria which would compete with industrial
production in Japan. He voiced the argument generally
current

=== Page 267 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
current here that Manchuria should play only the role
of supplier of raw materials to Japan and consumer of
rinished goods produced in Japan. The Finance Minister
stated that in general the Government concurred in
this view of the economic role of Manchuria and
indicated that China was to be cast in the same role.
Mr. S. Tsuda defended the establishment of new cotton
mills in China to replace those destroyed at Tsingtao
and Shanghai. He intimated that if these mills were
not rebuilt, there was danger of British attempts to
get a foothold. Mr. Ikeda recognized that the instability
of Chinese currency and linkage of the yen to Chinese
currency in China contributed to the difficulties of
Japanese exporters doing business with China, and
expressed a belief that currency abuses in China
would be rectified with the firm establishment of the
new Federal Reserve currency in north China. Mr. Furuta
of the Sumitomo Metal Works, Limited, declared that
governmental demands on essential industries for war
materials should not so overtax them as to impair their
future productive capacity, and that industrial control
plans should not be formulated solely by theorists.
Mr. Fujii of the Osaka Chamber of Commerce and Industry
called for enforcement of exchange and export controls
in north China in order to restrict the business
activities of aliens, and to insure a smooth source of
supply

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
supply of raw materials to Japanese consumers.
Mr. Ono, of the Finance Bureau of the Finance
Ministry, indicated thet a greater measure of economic
control in north China would be imposed as rapidly as
practicable.
Newspaper comment on Minister lkeda's speech and
the conference which followed indicated general approval
of his statements and policies. The JAPAN CHRONICLE
stated editorially in its issue of December 8,
"However divided national opinion may have been
prior to July, l937, there can be no doubt that after
seventeen months of hostilities the unity which sprang
into being then, remains undismayed."
The OSAKA MAINICH (December 7, 1938) praised the
Minister for his frank and fair statement of Japan's
financial condition but intimated that there might
be some cause to discount Mr. Ikeda's optimism regard-
ing the consumption of national bonds. "However,"
said the paper, "since the Finance Minister is at
ease on the situation, we had best accept his inter-
pretation, at least for the time being."
Both the Mainichi and the ASAHI (December 8)
expressed disappointment at the Minister's vagueness
in his discussion of the application of the Mobilization
act and future measures to be effected by the Government.
The ASAHI insisted that if the period of protracted
reconstruction was to entail further restrictions the
. general

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.97)
- 8 -
general public had a right to know the specific plens
which the Government had in mind, so that preparations
for greater sacrifices might be made.
In sunmary, the Osaka leaders manifested a keen
interest in the economic exploitation of Manchuria
and north China which is regarded as especially vital
to western Japan. They showed little regard for the
more abstract reesons &dvenced in other quarters for
the conquest of China. They displayed some misgivings
as to the possible invocation of Article ll of the
National Mobilization Act, end felt that their fears
were justified when both Mr. Ikeda and Mr. Yuki made
veiled hints thet the entire article might be enforced.
The consensus of opinion among them was that either
forced absorbtion of Government bonds, or forced
extension by them of credit to small industry would
affect their interests adversely. Their present
support of the Konoye Cabinet is undoubted, and
they are reluctantly resigned to present control
and taxation policies as necessary expedients.
Respectfully yours,
Auo A Makimon

DIVISION OF FAR EABTERN AFFAIRS
January 24, 1939
994-51/610
The conclusions expressed in the
economic analysis contained in Chungking's
47 of January 23, 10 a.m., that Japan's
position 1s becoming more vulnerable to
eoonomio retaliation on the part of the
western powers than superficial estimate
would indicate does not appear to be borne
out by Tokyo's 56 of January 14 containing
an eoonomic summary in regard to Japan for
1938 prepared by the Commercial Attaché.
The Tokyo Commercial Attaché's
summary concludes that, although the effeots
of eighteen months of hostilities are
undeniable, and although Japan's economic
control program has brought about many
adverse conditions, the solidity of the
economic system of the country has thus
far not been seriously undermined, and
the general internal financial situation
has not been unduly strained by the
unprecedented absorption of national bonds
nor have the living conditions of the
people been seriously impaired.
One result decidedly favorable to
Japan has been the marked expansion of the
productive power of the nation. The
intensified efforts directed toward self-
sufficiency have caused great expanslon in
the output of such products as aluminum,
pulp, magnesium, ammonium sulphate, steel
alioys,

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
1loys, alcohol, and certain synthetio
terials such as marine leather, rubber,
artificial wool from soya beans, and various
textile and fuel substitutes.
The increase 1n Japan's domestic output
of many commodities essential for war pur-
poses will thus contribute to easing Japan's
difficulties caused by the decline in her
foreign exchange resources which 1s caused by
a marked decrease in exports to countries
outside the yen bloc. It is noteworthy that
there has been a marked falling off in Japan's
1mports from the United States of most poten-
tial war materials, the two principal excep-
tions being mineral oils and copper.
The foregoing observations must be taken
into account in estimating the possible effects
of unilateral action by the United States in
applying economic sanctions against Japan and
lead the writer of this memorandum to the con-
clusion that if action is to be teken which 1s
likely to exert decisive pressure on Japan,
there must be envisaged at least far-reaching
measures taken jointly by a combination of
countries, which would include the United States,
the British Empire, France and the Netherlands.
Tokyo's economic summary appears to
provide a more comprehensive basis for ap-
praising the situation than the analysis,
chiefly financial, contained in Chungking's
telegram. However, Chungking's telegram
discusses

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.96)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
disousses more fully than does Tokyo's the
condition of Japan's foreign exchange resources
and it would therefore seem desirable to have
Tokyo's comments on Chungking's telegram
under reference. With this thought in mind,
a telegram has been drafted to the Embassy at
Tokyo.

February 23, 1939
Tokyo's despatch no: 3643 of January 30,
1939, subject "Speech of Finance Minister
Sotaro Ishiwata Before the House of Repre-
sentatives of the 74th Diet".
Mr. Ishiwata does not enlighten us in his
speech before the Diet. The facts have been
already reported and the interpretation is,
of course, optimistic. You may wish to read,
however, enclosure 2 to the despatch, it being
an editorial in The Japen Chronicle. The
editorial seems to be sound in its interpreta-
tion; however, it does not look beyond the
time when emergency expenditures must stop
and Japanese expended heavy industrial plant
will have to find peace-time markets for its
productsin a contrecting rather than expand-
ing economy.

I have the honor to forward herewith the English
22,1929
text
as printed in the JAFAN ADVERTISER of the speech
he Finance Minister, Ir. Sotaro Ishiwata, before the
Diet, su mitting the 1939-40 budget estimates and presenting
an explanation of Japan's economic conditions.
The general draft budget for the coming fiscal year
represents an increase of ¥180,000,000 over that for 1938-39
and will amount to over ¥3,694,000,000. 0rdinary revenues
F/A
are to be increased by ¥294,000,000 as compared with the
previous year,to be obtained primarily through increased
taxes; while a deficit of ¥1,082,000,000 is to be made up
by bond issues. Mention is made of a considerable saving
to

=== Page 297 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
to the Government by a retrenchment of ¥317,000,000 due to
a drop "in the cost of transporting troops to the front and
curtailment and postponement of projects of various ministries."
These figures, however, only pertain to the ordinary
general budget and it is stated that a "special supplementary
budget" will also be submitted to the current session of the
Diet to cover the military expenditures connected with the
"Chine incident". No mention is made as to how much will be
required for this special budget, but in an article quoted
from the NICHI NICHI it is intimated that a sum of some six
billion yen is being contemplated by the naval and military
ministries as being recuired for their needs.
In foreign trade a totel of ¥5,732,000,000 wes reached
and a favorable balance obtained of exports over imports of
¥60,000,000. This favorable balance was pointed out as a
remarkable achievement when compared with that of 1937 in
which there was an adverse balance of over ¥636,000,000.
The rate of exchange of the yen has been maintained and
in this connection the minister stated: "The Government considers
it appropriate in view of economic conditions at home and abroad
to maintain the existing foreign exchange level".
In discussing the economic situation of Japan the Finance
Minister declared that,with the remarkable progress made in
recent years, despite the "China Incident" our country is on a
solid foundation". He said that the Government, with the
poople's understanding and cooperation, had during the incident
satisfactorily assured itself of sufficient capital through
readjusting supply and demand and encouraging thrift. The daily
bank note issue during 1938 was estimated at ¥1,979,000,000,
showing

=== Page 298 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
showing an increase of over ¥384,000,000 over 1937, not
in his opinion, an unreasonable increase. There had been
an increase in savings and deposits during the year with
bank deposits showing a ¥3,370,800,000 yen rise and a
jump in postal savings of ¥689,000,000. Government bonds
in excess of ¥3,800,000 were purchased during the year.
At the same time "readjustment of supply and demand proceeded
smoothly" with over ¥2,800,000,000 invested through the
tentative Capital Re-Adjustment Act in enterprise necessary
to expand the country's industrial productivity and over
¥1,160,000,000 floated in the financial market.
However, to carry out "Japan's unshakable policy in
East Asia" and to continue on with the present developments,
the Minister explained that "our country is now faced with
a long period of construction work, destined to mark an
unprecedented development of our national fortunes". And
in order to accomplish this task it will be necessary, he
said, to replenish the nation's economic power and expand
the common economic capacity of Japan, Manchukuo and China.
To this end Mr. Ishiwata stressed first of all that
an increase of the industrial productivity of the three
countries is urgently needed. Means must be taken to expand
industry; but to facilitate such an expansion large funds
will be required to make the necessary capital invest-
ments. He said that the Government will bend every effort
to insure a steady supply of capital and he referred to the
savings of the people and the excess profits from successful
industrial concerns as the main sources from which these
future

=== Page 299 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
future funds primarily should be supplied.
At the same time, not only to obtain capital from
the people for investment in irdu strial enterprises but
as well to assure that the Government have whatever funds
it may need for its own new financing, continued thrift
of the country is demanded, and "the Government intends
to consider means of encouraging nation-wide savings".
Finally, efforts must be "redoubled to develop the
country's export trade from the standpoint of securing
funds for foreign commodities." "The Government has
already established a foreign exchange revolving fund
and taken other measures" -----"and intends to strengthen
and improve the measures already in force".
With the attention of the press focused primarily
on the addresses of the Prime Minister and the Foreign
Minister only limited attention appears to have been
given in the local papers to this speech. But the general
tone of the comments made was of approval of the budget
estimates and satisfaction of the picture of the economic
situation outlined by the Finance Minister.
An article in the MIYAko, in its issue of January 23,
is reflective of this tone when it stated "what is encouraging
about the diet speech of Finance Minister Ishiwata is the
faot that Japan's power is strong ----- and contrary to
the prediction of Foreign powers that Japan financially would
collapse before six months after the outbreak of the China
incident now that a year and a half has passed Japan has
actually shown that she has yet much of her financial
strength left."
The JAPAN CHRONICLE in its issue of January 24 published
21
an editorial, enclosed herewith, disoussing Japan's
currenoy

=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
ourrency situation and Government bond flotation since
the outbreak of the Sino-Japanese hostilities. The
editorial points out that the huge issues of Government
bonds, which have amounted to five billion six hundred
and thirty million yen since August l937 (four billion
three hundred and twenty million yen of which were issued
last year) have been thus far successfully absorbed without
serious adverse effect on the yen. Moreover, the amount
of yen outstanding in circulation has not increased
appreciably, the excess having been taken up by the Govern-
ment and by savings of the people, and there seems to be
no sign of the dangers of uncontrolled inflation arising.
On one point the speech of the Tinance Minister has
been questioned. The YOMIUlI, in an editorial published
on January 23, refers to the stabilizing of the yen at its
present level i.e. pegged to the pound sterling at ls.2d.
It is asked why this should form a basic exchange policy
for Japan and it is stated that there is no definite
or absolute reason why the yen must be linked to the pound
sterling - this notwithstanding a strenuous campaign on
the part of the Government to explain that the pegging
of the yen to sterling, "which may appear to be derogatory
to Japan's dignity", is made unavoidable by London being
the world's principal money market. The editorial then
mentioned that Mr. Ishiwata pointed out that the maintenance
of the present exchange level was most appropriate but contends
that the faot must not be overlooked that "there are men
directly interested in foreign trade who are advocating
devaluation".
The

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
The figures given by the Finance Minister for the
country's foreign trade during l938 show a favorable balance
of exports over imports to all countries of over sixty
million yen. However, the attainment of this favorable balance
may not necessarily demonstrate as strong and prosperous
economic well-heing of the country as seemingly is implied
by a reference to this favorable balance. For such a
favorable balance must be considered in the light of the
fact that it was gained only by a total drop of imports of
one billion five hundred and forty million six hundred and
thirty thousand and of exports of four hundred and twenty-two
million one hundred and thirteen thousand -- figures which
were released officially by the Foreign inister on January
10. Moreover it is also noted that Japan's favorable
balance of trade with the yen bloc amounted to over six
hundred million yen which, taking into consideration any
re-export from that bloc to other foreign countries, means
an estimated unfavorable balance of trade to the rest of
the world of over five hundred million yen. As, for the
present at least, it is from the rest of the world that a
large part of Japan's essential imports must come,this
unfavorable balance no doubt explains the stress placed
by the Foreign Minister on the necessity to increase the
nation's export trade.
Respeçtfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

WHAT IS JAPAN'S FINANCIAL POSITION?
The following expert analysis of Japan's financial
position has been prepared specially for X-Ray subscribers
in view of the serious considerati on now being iven both
in London and Washington to possible measures of economio
retaliation against Japan on account of her discrimination
against third-party interests in China. It is important to
judge how sensitive Japanese finances are likely to be to
such retaliation. The figures given below throw light on
this problem and also what has been the effect of oighteen
months of war on Japan's financial position.
Japanese Balance Sheet Effects of Eighteen Months of War.
The financial position of Japan has fallen considerably
in the world measured by Lodon stock exchange quotations of
the imperial Japanese Government's sterling loan of 1924
which is reprasentative of the other of Japan's stock, and
is now lower by 43% than it was before the outbreak of the
war in China if the actual yield of national loans in foreign
markets is a yard stick by which the comparative financial
standing of different countries can be measured.
Japan today rnks not only lower than China but at the
bottom of a lot of countries the finanoial reputation of
which is by no means of the best. To give but a few examples
of recent date, Bulgaria's 8-1/2% loan yields sterling 7. 1.,
Grecce's 6 loan yields sterling 8. 0. 0., Poland:s 7% loan
yields sterling 8. 9. 3., China's 5% 1913 loan yields 9. 0. 0.,
Germany's

-8
Gormany's 5 Dowes Loan yields 9. 12. 3., Japan's 6% 1924 loan
yields 1l. 0. 0. Among London's active foreign government
loans, only the 8% Czeohoslovakian loan and the 4% Roumanian
loan rank lower than the 6% loan of Japan. Although Japan has
never defaulted in her modern finanoial history the stook ex-
change barometer refleots only conorete economic facts, and
an estimate of coming ovents as a reading of Japanese condi-
tions may be unduly pessimistio in the near view, but there is
no doubt that Japan's international financial position is
steadily deteriorating and that a strain is being placed upon it
The trend of Japan's balance of internati onal payments is
an important barometer. Visible foreign trade is the most
important item in such a national balance sheet. In the c ase
of Japan the figures have to be estimated and cannot be gleaned
from the sum totals of the official returns which are broad-
oast over the world for these returns contain Japan's exports
to and imports from the other areas of the so-called 'yen
bloot which to all intents and purposes constitutes nothing
but domestic Japanese trade, involving the shifting of paper
yon but not of foreign exohange balanoes. These figures
falsify the pioture and have to be aliminated.
Japan's average monthly foreign exports other than to
countries in which the yen or yen-dependent currencies are th
medium of financial transactions with Japan, amounted to yen
120,700,000 for the first ten months of 1938, yen 216,000,000
for the same pend od of 1937 end yen 175,200,000 for the same
period of 1936. In other words Japan's forein exchange in-
come from exports in 1938 was 1ower by 44.2% in 1937 and by
31.2% compared with 1936.

The Colonial Surplus
Japan has the politiol means to appropriate to herself
from her colonial and other dependenoies making up the yen
bloo, whatever foreign exchange surpluses they may have in
their own dealings wi th the outside world just as she has to
foot the bill in case they produced defioits in their inter-
course with foreign countries. This item is the first in the
balance of Japan's invisible trade which requires analysis.
The Japanese colonies (Formosa, Korea and the South Sea Islard s)
according to figures for the first ten months of l938, may
realize a surplus in foreign exchange income from their direot
trade with foreign countries to the amount of aout yen
15,000,000 during the year against deficits of about yen
20,000,000 in 1937 and yen 55,000,000 in 1936. There the ex-
ports have been foroed up considerably while their imports
have been kept as low as possible in a great effort to do away
wi th the defioits.
Manchoukuo however has suffered a severe reverse in her
net foreign exohange income from trade with countries other
than those of the yen bloo. In 1936 she had seoured a net
export surplus in foreign exohange equivalent to yen 159,700,000
and in 1937 her export surplus still amounted to yen 113,400,000.
This was the time when China still being outside the yen bloc,
paid in foreign exchange for the huge import surplus which her
trade showed with her former North-Eastern provinces. These
balances whioh Japan, of course, laid hands upon, contributed
considerably to the stability of the yen. Now that the area
of China is being force into the yen bloo, these foreign ex-
change revenues are, judging from Manohoukuo's foreign trade
returns for the first seven monthe of l938, far less. The
year will probatly olose with a slight deficit of about

The Surplus frem China
There remains the question of Japan's indir ect fr eign
exchange revenue in l938 from the occupied territories of China
prooured by means of the forcible exchange of puppet and yen
notes into Chinese national dollars and by the purohase with
them of sterling, gold dollars, etc. It is, of course, im-
possible to obtain a reliable estimate of these amounts but a
guess plaoes them between Chinese dollars 25,000,000 and
50,000,000. At sterling parity (that is, the same amounts in
yen) the actual figure can soaroely have been any higher and
it was most probably on the low of this range. This would mean
that Japan has not succeeded in realizing by way of acquisition
of foreign exohange in China more than between 5% and 10% of
the combined favourable balance of Japan, her colonies and
Manchoukuo in their trade with the oocupied territories. This
total export surplus will reach about yen 250,000,000 for the
whole of 1938.
It would seem that Japan's total net foreign exchange
revenues from the respective vemvuroes of Korea, Formosa, Man-
choukuo and the ocoupied areas of China in l938, may have been
between yen 35,000,000 and yen 60,000,000. This is a consider-

foreign exchange benefits from the Japanese colonies and Man-
choukuo alone had amounted to yen 93,000,000 in 1937, and to
yen 105,000,000 in 1936, that is, during the years in which
China still paid in foreign exohange for her import surplus
with the little yen bloo of those days.

Items in the Balanoe
The last official Japanese returns of the balance of inter-
national payments for 1936 show an excess of expenditure over
income on two unspecified accounts, other current items and
government expenditures not otherwise indicated, amounting to
yen 40,200,000 and yen 127,500,000 respectively. They include
such items as payments for direct purchases abroad by the army
and navy, expenditures in foreign countries of secret funds,
payment for foreign patent rights and licenses, etc. It may
be roughly estimated that about one-third of these miscellaneous
exoess payments were made in Manohoukuo and need not be con-
sidered in this context but to the extent of at least yen
100,000,000 those obligations have probably also been incurred
in 1937 and 1938.

How the Aoocounts were Squared
In l936 the defioit on ourrent acoount of about yen
230,000,000 was squared by the virtual liquidation of all the
acoumulated foreign exohange holdings which Japan still retain-
ed at that time. In this way gold exports could still be
aveided. In l9s7 however when no more liquid reserves in
foreign exohange were available, gold sales to the United ststes
had to fil1 in the gap of yen 882,600,000. Japan did not
publish

=== Page 317 === (confidence 0.98)
-10-
publish any figures on her actual gold shipments, but the gold
import returns of the United States for l937 show imports from
Japan to the equivalent of yen 880,000,000. In 1938 larg•
gold exports had to be made once more. Up to August, 1938,
the United States received about yen 380,000,000 of Japanese
gold and shipments went on during the rest of the year. The
whole of the l938 deficit will not have been squared by gold
exports for in her dire need to retain at least some of her
gold reserves which during the first months of the war had
already deoreased considerably, Japan seems to have tried hard
to alleviate her difficulties in the only other way which was
still open to her by reducing the time-lag between payments
and reoeipts.
During the last year Jepan reduced the time lag between
her payment abroad and her own income from foreign countries.
This was one of the objectives of the inoreased state control
over foreign trade and especially of the system of linking
imports wi th exports introduced during the past year where
cash or at least short term payments had to be made for pur-
chases abroad in order to secure the lowest possible price for
imported goods. Longer terms are now more frequently being
demanded. Where on the other hand very liberal terms of
oredit used to be given to foreign oustomers of Japanese firms,
considerably shorter ones are now being adhered to wherever
possible. Moreover the tightening of Japan's foreign exohange
regulations served to "freeze" in Japan a volume of obligations
toward foreign countries. Practices like these, of course,
cannot be repeated, nor can their applioation be expected
fundamentally to alter Japan's financial situation. They por-
tend inereasing embarrassment in the future but considering
the magnitude of Japan's accounts with foreign countries, the
timo-1e

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
time-lag between payment made and payments received is bound
once, but not more than onoe to alleviate the pressure of an
unfavourable balance of internati onal paymen ts. This seems
to have been the case in l938 and to the extent that the
minimum time-lag between income and outgo has now been reduced.
It means the exhaustion of yet another of the few Japanese
reserves of financial strength.
Prospects for 1938.
It seems that Japan's balance of international payment
in the new year is bound to be such that her foreign exohange
difficulties will be inoreased still further rather than
diminished. Export sales to foreign countries can hardly be
expected to grow appreciably in spite of all efforts. It is
not even certain that they can be maintained at the present
reduced level. Stocks of imported raw materials which form
such a large part of what Japan's export goods are made from,
are becoming scarcer all the time. The "mobilization" of
Japan's economy as a whole is destined to be accelerated rather
than diminished even if the major hostilities in China should
be ended by some kind of peace, for Japan is preparing for
other "potential" enemies.
Price developments are strongly against Japan. Even the
maintenance of the comparatively low level of exports during
1938 was only made possible by further saorifices in export
prices. It is true that Japan managed under great difficulties
and resulting in some loss of trede to raise them. The
average "unit prioes of export goods" in 1938, according to
the oaloulations of the Yokohama Specie Bank, were higher by
13.9% than they had been in 1936, but even then they lagged
behind the rising cost of produotion for the foreign materials
whioh

=== Page 319 === (confidence 0.99)
-12
whioh play such a large role in the mamufacture of Japanese
export produots. The average rose by 24.3% during the same
time and the level of domestio wholesale prioes is high er now
by 40% than 1t was in 1936.
Imports from abroad oan hardly be decreased any further
for even their continuance at the present low level (30.3%
less in volume tonnage than in l937) would cause serious
diffioulties of supply not only for civilian domestic needs
and for the export industries but even for Japanese war economy
itself. The pre-war stooks of important raw materials were
used up to a large extent during the first fourteen months of
the present war from July, 1937, to September, 1938, (months
for which figures are available). Stocks of raw cotton de-
oreased by 67.7%, those of wool by 65.5% and those of American
1umber by 72.16%.
There is every indication that similar inroads have been
made into the stooks of raw materials for the produotion of
arms and ammunition which were no longer divulged while con-
sumption of these materials has grown rapidly and while the
produotion of such materials in Japan and in other members of
the yen bloo has not risen to anything like the same extent.
Imports from abroad showed a marked decrease in volume during
1938. The offioial tonnage figures for the average monthly
imports of minerals and mineral and metal manufaotures from
January to Ootober, 1938, were lower by 62.9% and 13.1%
respeotively than they had been in 1937 and 1936.
Japan's Vulnerability
No more than about yen 200,000,000 of the newly mined gold
will be available for squaring the l939 deficit on ourrent
account. To oover any of the defioit in exoess of this figure
Japan

=== Page 320 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
Japan will have to sell gold from her remaining stocks. These
can hardly be higher now than the yen 500,000,000 (in paper
values) which the Bank of Japan's return shows. If the total
deficit on current account in l939 remains about the same as
in 1938, all the national gold reserves would probably be
exhausted. Japan would then be left in a state of complete
dependence on foreign credits unless she were willing to out
down her military ventures and her fresh armaments to the very
low level determined by her ability to purchase foreign raw
materials against her own "visible" exports.
It is doubtless still premature to talk of Japan's imminent
"breakdown" but the foregoing caloulations seem to indicate
that Japan's position is becoming very vulnerable to economio
retaliation on the part of the Western Powers, muoh more so
indeed than a superficial estimate would indicate.

10. After the said series of loan bonds were

14. After the death of the decedent a most com-

States Government and other foreign securities hereinbefore
described, for the existence thereof was well known at the

vain and the results were fruitless.

ature of the ecoentricities of the decedent, the manner in

stranger, coming into the possession of said bonds surrep-
titiously or otherwise, might present the same for payment
or would possibly present coupons attached to said bonds for
redemption or payment.
17. After the maturity date of the said bonds, the

WILLIAM A. SINDELAR, being duly sworn, deposes and
says: I am a resident of the City of Cleveland, in the
County of Cuyahoga, State of Ohio, and am fully familiar
with all the facts connected with this proceeding.
Oliver D. Hovey, the decedent above named, was
at the time of his death, a resident of the City of Palmer,
in Hampden County, in the Commonwealth of Massachusetts,
and he departed this life on January 29th, 1918, leaving cer-
tain personal property.
The Union Trust Company of Springfield, Lassachu-
setts, was duly appointed executor under his Last Will and
Testament, which was duly admitted to probate on or about
April 3rd, 1918, by the Probate Court of the County of Hamp-
den, in said State, which Last Will und Testament had been
executed on January 8th, 19l4. by decedent.
The cril il

bate proceedings in the said state.
By the provisions thereof, one George Forest Hovey,
a nephew of the decedent, who resides at Chesterland, Ohio,
was named as sole legatee. No other person is interested
in the estate.
The Union Trust Company of Springfield, Massachusetts
continued to act as Executor under the terms and conditions
of the said Last will and Testament, for a number of years.
It filed an accounting, and resigned, and on August 24th,
1936, Letters of Administration de bonis non with the Will
annexed, upon the said estate of said decedent, were duly
issued out of said Probate Court in liassachusetts, to me.
I have ever since been acting and still am acting in that
capacity.
Thereafter, by a designation in writing, H. Bernard
Cohen, the petitioner herein, was duly authorized to act in
the State of New York, as said administrator de bonis non
with the Will annexed of said estate, and an exemplified copy
of the Will and cf the decree, admitting the same to probate,
and of the Letters issued to me, evidencing my appointment,
were all duly filed in the Surrogate's Court, New York County,
on January 1lth, 1938, With the petition for the appointment
of Mr. Cohen, as ancillary administrator de bonis non, c.t.a.

one of the Surrogates of the tounty of Low York, in the

of said court.
I make this affidavit in support of the petition
in this proceeding, which I understand is about to be filed
by the ancillary administrator. While acting as administra-
tor, I made a very thorough investigation and found that
during the life time of the said decedent, he had purchased
through his brother, Albert G. Hovey of Springfield, Massa-
chusetts, and through a broker in Springfield, Massachusetts,
various U. S. Government bonds, and also foreign Government
bonds.
This appeared by the testimony which was given by
the decedent's brother aforesaid, at a hearing held in said
Probate court at or about the time when the will was admitted:
to probate. After the death of the decedent, it was dis-
covered through investigations which had been made by the
Executor originally appointed and its attorney, as appears
by papers which came into my hands, that a series of Imperial
Japanese bonds were purchased by the decedent through Henry
H. Skinner, who was a banker or broker, in the City of
Springfield, Mass.
Said investigation revealed the identity of the
bonds, the dates of the purchase, the serial numbers thereon,
the denomination thereof and maturity dates. It was found
that these were all bonds of the Japanese Government, known
as 4 Sterling loan of 1905, maturing January 1st, 1931,
and the total principal represented by said bonds appear
to be 14,000., or thereabouts.
-3-

=== Page 364 === (confidence 0.99)
The decedent had purchased other bonds issued
by the Republic of France, known as French Republic two year
5 secured loan convertible gold notes, which were approx-
imately $5,000. in par value.
The decedent had been a man of some eccentricity,
and had been committed to a State Institution, on or about
November 5th, 1917, or a little more than two months prior
to his death. In fact, the Massachusetts courts had ap-
pointed one Benjamin B. Green of North Wilbraham, Massa-
chusetts, as Guardian for the decedent, on the ground that
he was incompetent and insane at the time. These foreign
bonds were in addition to other bonds that had been pur-
chased by the decedent.
After his death, a drastic search was made for the
purpose of locating the said bonds among his papers and
effects, but the Executor was unsuccessful, and from in-
quiry made and evidence ascertained, it is quite certain that
the decedent buried the said bonds on a farm which he fre-
quented, in or about the Village of wilbraham, Massachusetts.
Searching parties were organized who endeavored to locate
said foreign bonds, but without success. Apparently, the
Executor decided that the best thing to do was to wait until
the maturity date of the bonds arrived, so that the full
period of time would elapse within which some stranger who
might have found the bonds, could present the same meanwhile
for the payment of the interest coupons attached thereto,
end eventually for payment of the principal, at maturity.
Said Executor however, after 1931, either over-
looked the matter or became lax in the presentation and
socution or the estate's rights upon said bonds, and be-
of dissatisfaction by the said sole legatee, the Trust

DISTRICT OFFICE RELEASE
Bureau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce
June 6, 1938
To:
All District Offices
From:
Finance Diviaion
Subject:
CONFIRMED IRREVOC BLE LETTER OF CREDIT ADVISABLE ON
SHIPMENTS TO JAPAN
In viow of all the factors involved it appears advisable that exporters
should have a confirmed irrevocable letter of credit in their hands bofore
acoepting orders for shipments to Japan.
The procurement of exchange permits in Japan is beconing increasingly
diffioult and is a matter of growing ccncern to Jaanese importers. It is
understood that certain well-established Japanose firms have recently con-
tracted mith American exportors on the basis of paymont boforo arrival of the
goods in Japan. Japanese importers do not appoar to be warranted in mnking
promises of this nature. They have no meane of forotolling in advanco whother
or not their applications for exchance permits will bo granted. Private
Japaneso importers are obvioualy not in a position to bind the Ifinistry of
Finanoe in this mattor. Although it is understood that certain contracts of
this nature have been succesafully consummated in tho past, such instances
afford no assurance as to the future. In fact, one case hos just como to
our attontion where the Japanese importer has boen unable to obtain an oxchange
permit and carry out his obligation.
It should aleo be understood that an import pormit is not an exchange
permit and that possossion of the former con stitutes no guarantee that the
latter will be granted. There appears to have been some confusion on this
point.
In view of the increasing severity with which the exchenge rostrictions
in Japan are being enforced, 1t is believed that only a confirmed irrevocable
letter of credit offers the Ameriean exporter satisfactory assurance that the
Japanese importer has'fully complied with the regulations and that payment
will be duly forthcoming.
Amos E. Taylor, Acting Chief
amozE
Forwarded!
A
Robort Sevey, Chief.

From the foregoing table it will be noted that
during 1937 gold exports from Japan amounted to $251
million, and the decrease in net foreign exchange posi-
tion with other than yen-bloc countries is estimated at
$48 million, a total of $299 million. In Part II of
this memorandum the deficit in the Japanese current
international account with other than yen-bloc countries
for 1937 is estimated at 982 million yen, or $283 mil-
lion, which is quite close to the foregoing estimate of
gold exports plus decrease in net foreign exchange posi-
tion. It may be noted that these two estimates were
arrived at independently.
The deficit in Japan's current international account
with other than yen-bloc countries for l938 1s estimated
at from $176 to $184 million. From the foregoing table
it may be noted that during 1938 gold exports less gold
under earmark at the end of the year amounted to $152
million, while the decrease in net foreign exchange
position is estimated at $43 million, a total of $195
million. In the case of the estimates for l938, however,
the estimated deficit in the balance of payments was used
in arriving at the estimated decrease in net foreign
exohange position since the independent data on which
this particular estimate is based is not yet available.
Conclusions

=== Page 385 === (confidence 0.99)
4
SIRICILY CONFIDENTIAL
Conclusiong
If it is assumed that during the next few years the
deficit 1n the Japanese current international account
with other than yen-bloc countries is no greater thanin
1938 and that acquisitions of gold continue at about
the same rate as during 1938, so that the net decrease
in internationally realizable assets continues at the
rate of $ll9 million a year, the foregoing estimates
indicate that the internationally realizable assets of
Japan would not be exhausted for approximately four
years.
Although Japan will probably be unable to continue
for long the acquisition of old gold at the rate
assumed for 1938, it is probable that increased gold
and silver production will in large part offset a
decreasing ability to obtain old gold. It is not at
all certain, however, that during the next few years
the deficit on current international account will not
exceed that of the past year. On the one hand it may
be argued that stocks of raw materials in Japan are low
and that rising costs are making it more difficult to
sell in world markets. As against this, however, Japan
has not exhausted the possibility of subsidizing exports
and may, if pressed hard enough, reduce imports still
more

=== Page 386 === (confidence 0.99)
5
more. Japan may also be able to exploit to better advan-
tage at least some of the large part of China under her
control. Obviously estimates such as the foregoing can-
not take into account developments such as war between
other major powers and Japan.
Finally, it may be noted that although exports of
gold have exoeeded acquisitions of gold by only $5
million during the first four months of 1939, there has
been a reduction of $32 million in gold under earmark
since the beginning of the year. Consequently estimated
gold reserves have been reduced from $252 million at the
end of 1938 to $215 m1llion at the end of last April.

MEMORANDUM
JAPANESE GOLD AND FOREIGN
EXCHANGE RESOURCES
一
PART II - SUPPORTING DATA
I
JAPANESE GOLD RESERVES
A memorandum prepared in this office on February 2,
1939, which carried forward earlier EA memoranda on this
subject, estimated Japanese gold reserves as of January
25, 1939 at 218 million dollars. By the end of February
this estimate would be reduced by gold arrivals in the
United States and shifts in earmarked gold to l9l million
dollars, of which 163 million dollars (50l mill1on gold
yen) would represent the gold reserves of the Bank of
Japan, 25 million dollars gold under earmark in the
United States, and 3 million dollars gold in the Gold
Fund Special Account of the Japanese Government or the
Exchange Revolving Fund of the Bank of Japan.
A study prepared by the Federal Reserve Bank of New
York, showing the estimated monetary gold stock of Japan
at the end of each month from August of 1937 to February
of 1939, estimated Japanese gold reserves as of the
latter

=== Page 388 === (confidence 0.97)
SIRICILY CONFIDENTIAL
-2
latter date at 22l million dollars, or 30 million dol-
lars in excess of the previous estimate by this office.
1. Japanese Gold Reserves as of August 25, 1937.
In the Federal Reserve study the monetary gold stock
of Japan on August 25, 1937 is estimated at 396 million
dollars. This estimate, which corresponds to that made
in earlier memoranda prepared in this office, was arrived
at as follows: In August of 1937 the Diet authorized
the devaluation of the yen from 750 milligrams of fine
gold to 290 milligrams, thus reducing the dollar equiva-
lent of the gold yen from its former mint par of 84.4
cents to 32.6 cents. The Japanese Government announced
that on August 25, 1937, the date on which devaluation
was made effective, the Bank of Japan would hold 450
million yen of gold at the old valuation, which at the
new valuation would be equivalent to 1,164 million yen.
At the same time the gold reserves of the Bank of Chosen
and Taiwan, amounting to 19 million yen, old valuation,
or 49 million yen at the new valuation, were transferred
to the Government. The monetary gold stock of Japan on
August 25, 1937 would therefore appear to have been 1,213
million yen at the new valuation, or 396 million dollars.
Of this amount, 80l million yen at the new valuation
(or

=== Page 389 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
(or 26l million dollars) were left with the Bank of Japan
as the gold reserve of that institution, and 4l2 million
yen (or 135 million dollars) were transferred to the
Government's Gold Fund Special Account. This account
also received certain other assets in the form of Govern-
ment bonds and deposits, and was to be used to control
yen exchange, buy domestic gold, both newly mined and
privately held old gold, and regulate the Government
bond market. Since the establishment of this fund the
Government has guarded its operations with extreme
secrecy.
2. Lack of Official Information Concerning
Gold Reserve.
The only officially reported reserves are those of
the Bank of Japan, which have been reported as 8ol mil-
lion gold yen (26l million dollars) from August 25, 1937
to July 19, 1938, when the Government caused the transfer
of 300 million yen to a special Exchange Revolving Fund
to be used in financing the importation of necessary raw
materials. Since that time the officially reported
reserves have been 50l million yen (163 million dollars).
The Government of Japan does not publish information
whioh would permit any allocation of estimated unreported
reserves between this Exchange Fund and the Gold Fund
Speoial Aocount.
sinoe

=== Page 390 === (confidence 0.98)
4
Since August 25, 1937 it has been possible to obtain
acourate statistics on Japanese gold exports since so far
as is known, all exports have gone entirely to the United
States. Moreover, since July of 1938, Japan has had
varying amounts of gold under earmark in the United States,
and the Federal Reserve Bank of New York 1s naturally in
a position to know the exact amount of Japan's earmarked
gold.
The Japanese Government has not released any infor-
mation concerning newly mined gold purchased since the
end of 1937, and apparently has never 1ssued any statistics
as to old gold purchased. Both the Federal Reserve esti-
mates of acquisitions of gold and the estimates prepared
in this office are therefore subject to a considerable
margin of error, particularly in regard to purchases of
old gola.
3. Comparison of Federal Reserve Estimates
with Estimates of this Office.
As was mentioned above, the Federal Reserve estimate
of Japanese gold reserves as of the end of February of
1939 was 221 million dollars as against the previous
estimate of this office of l9l million dollars. This
discrepancy of 30 million dollars may be accounted for
as follows:
(1) Earlier

=== Page 391 === (confidence 0.99)
SIRICTTY CONFIDENUAL
-5
(1) Tarller estimates prepared in this office
assumed that 1l million dollars of gold exports made on
August 23 and 24 should be deducted from the 396 million
dollars of gold reserves which both this office and the
Federal Reserve Bank of New York assumed to be held by
Japan as of August 25, 1937. The Federal Reserve estimate
assumes that the reported gold reserves of that date
totaled 396 million dollars after deducting those shipments.
This factor has accordingly made earlier EA estimates of
Japanese gold reserves lower than the Federal Reserve
estimates by ll million dollars.
(2) EA estimates made no allowance for old gold
purchased during the last quarter of 1937, while the
Federal Reserve estimate makes an allowance (admittedly
a guess) of 15 million dollars. From this may be sub-
tracted 5 million dollars which EA estimated to have
been derived from this source during 1938, while no
allowance for old gold purchased is made in the Federal
Reserve estimate since the end of 1937.
(3) Since the beginning of 1938 EA's estimate of
newly mined gold purohased has been 5 million dollars a
month, or 70 million dollars. The Federal Reserve
estimate of newly mined gold purchased is 5 million dol-
lars a month until June of 1938 and 6 million dollars a
month

=== Page 392 === (confidence 0.99)
month thereafter, a total of 78 million dollars, or
8 million dollars in excess of LA's estimate.
(4) The Federal Reserve statistics of gold exports
are based on preliminary assay office estimates and are
not adjusted for final payment as were the statistics
used by IA. Over the whole period in question this leads
to an undervaluation of gold shipments by a little less
than 1 million dollars.
The total of these four items is 30 million dollars,
the amount of the discrepancy to be accounted for.
4. Revision of the Two Estimates.
Newly Mined Gold Purchased
I believe that EA's more conservative estimate of
newly mined gold purchased is somewhat more accurate than
the Federal Reserve estimate. Both estimates agree that
approximately 20 million dollars of newly mined gold
were purohased between August 25 and the end of 1937.
This checks with the fact that in January of 1938 the
Vice Governor of the Bank of Japan reported that 181
million yen of newly mined gold was purchased during
1937. At the official buying price of gold this would
be equivalent to approximately 54 million dollars. The
study prepared by the Federal Reserve Bank of New York
arrives

=== Page 393 === (confidence 0.97)
7心
arrives at the conclusion that new gold purohased from
January 1, 1937 to August 25, 1937 amounted to 962
thousand ounces. This estimate is based on the assump-
tion that 150,000 ounces of gold were purchased from
August 1 to August 25, whioh was approximately the rate
of acquisition during the preceding month, to which
should be added the 8l2 million ounces that the Govern-
ment reported to the Diet in August of 1937 had been
purohased during the first seven months of the year.
The dollar equivalent of 962 thousand ounces 1s approxi-
mately 34 million dollars. Deducting this from the 54
million dollars purchased during the entire year leaves
a balance of 20 million dollars, or the equivalent of
estimated newly mined gold purchased during the remainder
of the year.
As pointed out above, however, EA's estimate of
newly mined gold purchased during 1938 was 60 million
dollars. This approximately equals the most recent
estimate of the Commercial Attaché at Tokyo, who reports
that gold production during 1938 probably approached 200
million yen, which, at the Japanese official buying rate
for gold, amounts to nearly 60 million dollars. The
Federal Reserve estimate of 66 million dollars would
therefore appear a little high. On the other hand,
rocent

=== Page 394 === (confidence 0.98)
STHICTLY CONFIDENTIAL
-8
recent reports indioate that there has been an increase
in gold produotion during the last two months of the year
and suggest that production during 1939 may increase some-
thing like 20 percent over that of the preceding year.
Consequently during 1939 newly mined gold purchased may
average approximately 6 million dollars a month.
It would therefore appear that EA's estimate of
newly mined gold purchased might well be increased 2
million dollars to take account of possibly larger
acquisitions during the latter part of l938 and first
two months of l939, while the Federal Reserve estimates
should probably be reduced by about 6 million dollars.
Old Gold Purchased
The estimate about whioh there is the most uncer-
tainty is that for purchases of old gold. The Government
extended its official buying price for gold to gold
derived from coin in June of 1937 and has made a strong
offort to purchase old gold in the form of scrap gold,
plate and the like. The Federal Reserve Bank has esti-
mated that from the end of 1936 to August 25, 1937 the
Japanese government purchased about a million ounces, or
35 million dollars of old gold. This estimate was arrived
at by subtracting officially reported purohases of newly
mined gold and reported decrease in Bank of Japan reserves
from

=== Page 395 === (confidence 0.99)

from known gold exports.* I believe this estimate to be
fairly accurate since the Government had not yet entered
the period of complete secrecy as to gold purchases and
exports and there is no evidence that the statement of

In addition to the 35 million dollars of old gold
purchased prior to August 25, 1937, the Federal Reserve
study estimates that 15 million dollars of old gold was
purohased during the remainder of that year, though this
1s admittedly a guess.
The Federal Reserve made no allowance for old gold
acquired since the end of 1937 on the assumption that
the easily obtainable old gold had probably been acquired
and that the remaining old gold acquired would be negli-
gible in amount in the absence of Governmental efforts
of a ooercive nature. Recently, however, the Government
has intensified its efforts to obtain old gold and has
introduced a bill into the Diet which would compel the
sale of privately held gold.
I would be inolined to regard the Federal Reserve
estimate of 15 million dollars of old gold acquired in

nearly 30 million dollars.
Revision of EA Estimates
A careful review of the evidence leads to the conolu-
sion that EA's previous estimates of Japanese gold reserves
should be revised as follows:
(1) An increase of ll million dollars to correct
for gold exports made prior to August 25, 1937 whioh were
improperly treated as having occurred since that time.
(2) An

Revision of Federal Reserve Estimates
Conversely the Federal Reserve estimate might be
corrected as follows:
(1) Reduced by 1 million dollars for undervaluation
of gold exports.
(2) Reduced by 6 million dollars for overestimate
of newly mined gold purchased.
(3) Increased by 15 million dollars for old gold
purohased in excess of the 15 million dollars already
allowed for in the last quarter of 1937.
This would lead to a net increase of 8 million dol-
lars, whioh would raise the Federal Reserve estimate from
221 million dollars to 229 million dollars, or the same
amount as the revised EA estimate. It will be noted that
this represents an inorease of less than 4 percent in the
Federal Reserve estimate.

II
JAPANESE HOLDINGS OF
FOREIGN SECURITIES
Japanese-held foreign securities are supposed to be
registered with the Japanese Ministry of Finance, and
under Japanese exchange-control legislation the Govern-
ment has the power to mobilize these securities. Foreign
securities provide the Government of Japan with a poten-
tial emergenoy foreign exchange resource of some magnitude,
but it is difficult to arrive at an estimate of the value
of this resource.
Official Statistics of Japanese-Held Foreign Securities
The Japanese Ministry of Finance has not published
any statistics of Japanese-held foreign securities since
October of 1937 when statistics as of April 30, 1937
were released. These statistics do not provide for any
breakdown by countries. Consequently, although Chinese
and Manchurian State loans issued in Japan are excluded,
as well as corporate shares and bonds in yen placed in
Japan, it 1s possible that the offioial statistics
inolude a considerable amount of securities of yen-bloc
countries. This may be offset to some extent, however,
by the existenoe in Japan of privately held foreign
seourities not registered with the Ministry of Finance,
but

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.99)
-14
but which the Government might be able to mobilize if
an intensive effort were made.
As of April 30, 1937, Japanese-held foreign securi-
ties were officially valued at 389 million yen. This
valuation was at the old mint par of exchange (2.006 yen
to the dollar) and at the face value of the securities.
The nominal value of these securities is therefore
approximately 195 million dollars, consisting of 131
million dollars of State bonds, 5 million dollars of
municipal bonds, l0 million dollars of corporate bonds,
and 49 million dollars of stocks.
Previous Estimate by this Office
After consideration of the evidence then available,
a memorandum prepared in this office, under date of
January 13, 1938, reached the conclusion that foreign
corporate securities could largely be disregarded as a
possible source of foreign exchange, both because of
their probable overvaluation and beceuse they probably
consisted very largely of Chinese and Manohurian securi-
ties. Similarly foreign municipals were disregarded on
the ground that they probably consisted largely of
Chinese and Manchurian securities held by Japanese banks
and insurance companies operating in those countries.
Finally

=== Page 401 === (confidence 0.99)
15
Finally it was assumed that a large proportion of the
foreign State bonds could not be mobilized because 95
peroent were reported as held abroad and were presumably
held against foreign currency liabilities. The memorandum
concludes that because of these factors the Japanese Gov-
ernment could not realize more than 80 to 85 million
dollars from Japanese-held foreign securities. For rea-
sons that I shall indicate below, I believe this estimate
too conservative.
Securities Pledged as Collateral in New York
I am led to this conclusion in part by strictly con-
fidential information which I have secured concerning
borrowing by agencies of Japanese banks in New York from
American banks. This information was supplied by the
New York State Banking Authorities (which are in charge
of examination of the branches and agencies of Japanese
banks in New York) to the Federal Reserve Bank of New
York. For some time the main offices of Japanese banks
(particularly of the Yokohama Specie Bank) have had sub-
stantial overdrafts on their New York branches or agencies.
Since the end of last December these overdrafts have fluc-
tuated between 48 and 41 million dollars. At the same
time the New York agencies have borrowed from banks in
this country, plodging as collateral Japanese export
b111s

=== Page 402 === (confidence 0.99)
SIRICIIY CONFIDENTIAL
-16
bills on American importers, a substantial amount of
United States Government bonds, Home Owners Loan Corpo-
ration bonds, a small amount of United States Treasury
bills, Canadian Government bonds, and, it is believed,
a small amount of Japanese dollar bonds. Some high-
grade American corporates have also, I am informed, been
used as collateral.
The New York State Banking Authorities did not give
any details as to the amounts of the various kinds of
collateral, but it is believed that in view of the prob-
able insistence of American banks on an ample margin of
security, and in view of the banking practice of requir-
ing a considerable deposit balance to be maintained when
oredits are extended, the amount of collateral pledged
by the New York agencies of Japanese banks must have been
substantial. The officials of the Federal Reserve Bank
of New York with whom I discussed this matter seemed
impressed by the excellence of the collateral and expressed
the opinion that for the most part it did not consist of
securities owned by the New York agency of the Yokohama
Specie Bank and other agencies but of securities that had
been assembled by the Japanese Government and transferred
to the New York agencies to serve as collateral for oredits.
Moreover, although the Japanese have sometimes borrowed
against

=== Page 403 === (confidence 0.99)
-17-
against gold in transit or collateral, it was not believed
that this had been done to a large extent recently, so
that even though a large part of the collateral consisted
of export bills, a significant amount consisted of securi-
ties. It is probable that as much as 20 million dollars
of seourities has been pledged as collateral.
It is somewhat surprising that the Japanese Govern-
ment has been able to mobilize a large amount of foreign
securities without the matter becoming generally known.
However, official statistics indicate that approximately
95 percent of Japanese-held foreign government and
municipal bonds were held abroad where they would be
1mmediately available for sale or pledging as collateral.
Moreover, the large proportion of these securities held
abroad suggests that to a considerable degree they may
have been concentrated in the hands of a few large
Japanese enterprises and could hence be mobilized both
quickly and secretly.

Seourities

seven

=== Page 406 === (confidence 0.99)
STRICTLY CONFIDEN:

Revised Estimate
In my opinion, while the Federal Reserve estimate
of the realizable value of Japanese-held foreign currency
securities is too high, the previous estimate prepared
in this Office is too low. The earlier estimate of this
Office offers no evidence to justify the assumption that
all of the Japanese-held municipal securities are Chinese
issues. In addition, I find the assumption that all
corporate securities should be rejected unconvincing.
The chief reason given for rejecting corporate securities
from the earlier estimates of this Office is that Remer
in his study of "Foreign Investments in China" estimated
Japanese investments in Chinese corporations at 77 million
yen in 1930, since when they are known to have increased.
It should be noted, however, that in his disoussion Remer
refers to most of these investments as "loans" and
"advances" to Chinese corporations, and the figures given
are always in yen. It is thus by no means sure that these
securities are of the type that would be included in the
offioial statistics of Japanese-held foreign securities;
a considerable amount of them might be yen issues or loans
and hence would be excluded from the official statistics.
It may also be noted that on December 23, 1937 the
Commercial Attaché at Tokyo reported that the amount of
Manchoukuoan

=== Page 407 === (confidence 0.97)
STRN
21-
Manohoukuoan shares expressed in Manohoukuoan currency,
and which might therefore be inoluded in the official
statistics, was only 23.5 million yen, while the official
statistics list the value of all Japanese-held foreign
shares at 97 million yen. Finally it seems to me that
there is a very strong presumption that, given the enor-
mous concentration of wealth in Japan, some of the wealthy
Japanese, for reasons of diversification of investment,
would have placed some of their funds in American or
European corporate bonds and shares.
The realizable value of the Japanese-held foreign
securities that the Japanese Government could mobilize
1s, of course, highly conjectural. Further consideration
of the available evidence, however, leads me to believe
that a reasonable maximum estimate would be l20 million
dollars, or about 24 million dollars less than the Federal
Reserve estimate. In other words, I believe that Japan
might well realize approximately 75 percent of the $136
million nominal value foreign governments and municipals,
or about $105 million, 50 percent of the $10 million
nominal value of corporate bonds, and perhaps $1o million
from its $49 million nominal value of foreign shares.

III
JAPANESE EXTERNAL BONDS
The published statistics of the Ministry of Finance,
as of April 30, 1937, state that the nominal value of
securities of Japanese external issues held by Japanese
nationals was 76l million yen at old par of exchange, or
approximately 380 million dollars. In my opinion these
securities do not constitute a potential foreign exchange
resource of very great magnitude. It is my understanding
that the market for these issues, in New York, at least,
is so narrow that any attempt to liquidate substantial
amounts would send prices to exceedingly low levels.
The officers of the Federal Reserve Bank in New York
with whom I discussed this matter had not heard of any
unusual activity in Japanese issues and believed that
it would have come to their attention had trading in
these 1ssues been active. Since the beginning of 1938
Japanese 6 1/2's of 1954 have fluctuated between 86 1/2
and 61 1/2 and are currently selling at 75. Japanese
5 1/2's of 1965 have fluctuated between 72 and 44 and
are currently selling at 55 1/8.
The Japanese may have secured some foreign exchange
by selling external issues, but since it is improbable
that much could be realized from this source in an emer-
genoy

=== Page 409 === (confidence 0.98)
-23
gonoy, I do not believe that earlier estimates of 25
million dollars made by this office need be changed. It
may be noted, however, that the Federal Reserve estimates
allow 69 million dollars as the realizable value of
Japanese external issues. Economic retaliation against
Japan would doubtless make these securities practically
worthless.
IV
SILVER
The amount of silver privately held in Japan 1s
unknown. The Director of the United States Mint esti-
mated the value of monetary silver in Japan (including
Chosen, Taiwan, Kwantung and Karafuto) as of the end of
1937 at 97 million dollars. A study prepared by an
officer of the Federal Reserve System in January of 1938
estimated the fine silver content of Japanese coin in
ciroulation at 2l0 million ounces, which at the average
value of silver in 1937 would be equivalent to 94 million
dollars. How much of this silver might be made available
for export is unknown, particularly since only a relatively
minor part of Japanese silver coin is concentrated in bank
reserves and in the past Japanese silver coin has been
exported to China and Manchuria. Nevertheless, Japanese
silver

=== Page 410 === (confidence 0.98)
STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL
-34
silver coin does represent an eventual if not immediate
foreign exchange resource of some magnitude. In this
oonnection it should be noted that an Imperial Ordinance
of June 1, 1938 provided for the 1ssue of 50-sen notes,
which of course might be used to displace 50-sen coin.
Recent reports indicate that this is now being done,
and the Government is making an effort both to increase
silver production and reduce domestic consumption.
The Federal Reserve study mentioned above estimates
the silver content of outstanding l-yen coin and trade
dollars at 126.1 million ounces, and that of the 50-een
coin at 84 million ounces. It is unlikely that much of
the coin of l-yen denomination 1s in active circulation;
most of it is probably hoarded or has been exported. The
withdrawal of silver coin will therefore probably be
limited to 50-sen pieces, which, at the ourrent price of
silver; have a bullion value of approximately 36 million
dollars. What proportion of the 50-sen coin in circula-
tion the Government will be able to collect, and how long
the process will take, is of course highly uncertain.
Silver production in the Japanese Empire may be
estimated at approximately l0 million ounces per annum.
During the last few years there has been a steady net
export of silver from Japan in excess of new production
within

=== Page 411 === (confidence 0.97)
-25
within the Empire. These exports have gone largely to
the United Kingdom and the United States. Thus exports
of silver from Japan to these countries amounted to
approximately 14 million ounces in 1937 and 17 million
ounces in 1938, the value of which was respectively 6.4
and 7.4 million dollars. Since the Japanese Government
no longer publishes statistics of import, production and
export of silver, it is not known to what extent exports
of silver to countries other than the United States and
the United Kingdom oocurred, nor the extent of silver
1mports into Japan.
It is probable that Governmental efforts to increase
silver production and reduce silver consumption, as well
as the attempt to substitute paper for silver coin, will
lead to a substantial increase in silver exports from
Japan during the next few years. In view of the fact
that the Bank of Japan has approximately 34 million yen
of coin and bullion other than gold, and in view of the
possibility that a considerable amount of 50-sen coin
may be obtained in exchange for notes, it would appear
that Japan might in the course of several years obtain
as much as 20 million dollars from available stocks of
silver coin and bullion.

JAPANESE EXCHANGE BALANCES
Federal Reserve Estimate
The Federal Reserve Bank of New York has made
estimates of the net foreign exchange position of the
principal Japanese banks, including the Bank of Japan,
based upon the detailed bank statements mentioned in II
above. By net foreign exchange position is meant the
excess of foreign exchange assets over foreign exchange
liabilities. The principal foreign exchange assets
included are exchange purchased and deposits in foreign
banks. The principal foreign exchange liabilities are
exchange sold and amounts due foreign banks. The net
foreign exchange position does not include the foreign
securities held by banks, which have been mentioned in
II above. The Federal Reserve study corrects the
estimated foreign exchange position for yen-bloc cur-
rencies, this correction being based in large part on
the ratio of yen-bloc trade to total trade.

exohange position of the twelve banks, as of the dates

It may be noted that the difference of 43 million

assumed to have been entirely balanced by the decrease
in net foreign exchange position, rather than by a

from foreign banks. (See seotion II above.) This has
been done for several reasons. In the first place,
securities pledged as collateral are still Japanese
assets until sold. In the second place, if Japanese
banks are following normal accounting procedure,
borrowing from foreign banks should result in an
increase in amounts due to foreign banks. This would
mean an inorease in the foreign exchange liabilities
of Japanese banks and would reduce their net foreign
exohange position by an equivalent amount once the
proceeds of the credits were expended, as is here
assumed to be the case.

expedients to whioh the Japanese have resorted to stem

banks, to lengthen the time lag between the date of
1mportation and actual remittance. In some cases the
Ministry of Finance has inserted stipulations in exchange
permits postponing remittances for six months to a year.
Consequently some American firms have exported to Japan
goods which are not even covered by forward exchange
contracts, since the usual maximum for such contracts is
six months. Again there have been increasingly severe
restrictions on the provision of exchange for remitting
profits, dividends and royalties.
In my opinion, therefore, the foregoing estimates
of the depletion of Japanese foreign exchange assets 1s
a reasonably conservative one. It is possible, however,
that in view of the inoreasing disposition of foreign

of credit basis, an excess of imports no greater than
that of 1938 may in the future lead to a somewhat larger

.seoretary:
You will reoall that on February 3,
1939, in response to questions raised by
the Department, the American Embassy in
London reported that British banks and
firms still extended to certein Japanese
interests short term oredits but on a
decidedly reduced soale; that old lines
of credit that were being maintained were
only a fraction of the amounts normally
extended and had been progressively re-
duood sinoe July 1937.
The Embassy now states that the situa-
tion is virtually the same as previously
reported; that all lines of credit are
being slowly but surely reduced; that Japan-
ese paper 1s now rarely seen in the London
discount market; that credits extended to
Japan are gradually approaching a minimum
level below which British banks and com-
meroial firms will be reluctant to contract
lest they lose old and reliable banking and
trading connections; a level which only
the foroe of national political events can
alter.

I have the honor to refer to the Department's
1939
612
instruction No.750, May 27, 1939, requesting that
894.51
February 3, 7 p.m.,regarding the extent to which
British banks and business firms are giving banking
F/FG
and commeroial credits to Japanese interests, be
brought up to date.
The general considerations set forth in that
telegram still hold good. All lines of credit are
being

=== Page 436 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
being slowly but surely reduced but it is impossible,
for the reasons previously given, to obtain exact
information of the amounts involved. Japanese paper
is now rarely seen in the London discount market, but
recently there has been some slight increase in
Australian agency bills which are drawn in connection
with sales of wool to Japan.
In general it can be said that credits extended
to Japanese commercial and banking interests are gradual-
ly approaching a minimum level below which British banks
and commercial firms will be reluctant to contract, lest
they lose old and valuable banking and trading connections.
Only the force of national political events can alter such
a minimum level. But if it becomes apparent to the City
that Japan is to pursue a policy of unrelenting hostility
to Great Britain, then the contraction may well follow
the course it took in the case of Italy and go as far as
possible.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:

Commerce. The chart on page 2 summarizing Japan's in-
