My dear Mr. Hackworth:
Yours of the 27th ultimo has been received and contents
noted.
I must in behalf of the ancillary administrator respect-
fully take exception to the refusal of the Secretary of State at
4
2
this time to submit the claim to the Imperial Japanese Government.
I must take the position that the rights of an American citizen
against a foreign government are being seriously jeopardized by the
position that the State Department is taking in declining to sub-
mit the claim and it is the opinion of the ancillary administrator
that if further delay occurs irreparable harm will result. It is
his opinion also that as time goes on the financial position of the
Imperial Japanese Government may possibly be such that moratoriums
mmy be declared, the effect of which will impede indefinitely the
payment of this just claim. The ancillary administrator takes the
position that it is not fair to allow this matter to lag in the De-
partment pending the disposition of the proceedings now before the
Surrogateis Court in New York. Every stumbling block that can be
put in the path of the ancillary administrator in the courts is
being done.
of course you know, as I have pointed out heretofore, that
the Imperial Japanese Government is not and cannot be made a party
in the proceedings in the Surrogate's Court. An entirely indepen-
dent proceeding as against its fiscal agent, The Yokohama Specie
Bank, Ltd., has been instituted. That banking institution has ap
peared through counsel specially and not generally and has challenged
the jurisdiction of the court to the other claim. The court has
taken the matter under advisement. I therefore now make the in-
quiry of you as to what your position will be if the Surrogate's
F
Court should decide that it has no jurisdiction of the claim in so
/WB
far as The Yokohama Specie Bank, Ltd. is concerned.

August 9th, 1939.
Secretary of State,
Washington, D. C.
Re: Estate of Oliver D. Hovey, Dec'd
Le 894.51/642
Honored Sir:-
Yours of the 17th ult. has been received and con-
tents noted. I have been continually trying to stress one partic-
ular point in this matter, and that is that the proceedings pending
in the Surrogates Court are not against the Imperial Japanese Govern-
ment. That proceeding has been brought against the Yokohama Specle
Bank, Ltd. fiscal agent of that Government. That banking instit-
ution has raised every conceivable technical point on the argument.
894.51/6
of the applioation, so as to avoid being brought into the jurisdic-
tion of the court, going so far as to contend that the claim of the
estate 1s not against the Bank, but against the Japanese Government,
and therefore may not be reviewed in the courts, because as a sover-
eign power, the Empire of Japan is immune from process and litiga-
tion.
Your repeated statements in said communication and
46
previous letters addressed to me, to the effect "that the alleged
right ** to receive the bonds in question should be determined 1n
appropriate judicial prooeedings" has been noted, and repetition of
the same does not help to clarify the situation. The Imperial Jap-
anese Government may not be brought into any judicial proceedings,
be it appropriate or otherwise, as you well know. I have no recourse
against that Go vernment in the courts of this state. My client's
FILED
recourse against that Government is through diplomatic channels, and
he demands that this Government present the claim to the representa-
tives ar the Empire of Japan accredited to this country, for coneid
eration.
3
9
The claim in my opinion 1s well set forth in the
petition heretofore filed. I must insist in my client's behalf that
that petition be presented to the Japanese Ambassador, or such other
official as you ordinarily present such olaims to. There is more than
sufficient in my opinion contained in the previous communications I
wrote to you and in the verified petition and affidavit lodged with
you to justify the presentaton of the claim. The claim, I believe
should be submitted to and passed upon by the Japanese Government,
and not by the Seoretary of State, and I say this with due deference.

=== Page 457 === (confidence 0.99)
Secretary of State
Page #2
Aug. 9/39
I respectfully request that you, the Seoretary of
State present this claim to the Japanese Ambassador for the ancil-
lary administrator of this estate, ithout further delay, and that it 1s for
a representative of said Government ask for evidence and proofs
further than are contained in the verified petition and affidavit
heretofore filed.
I have not requested the Department of State to
pass on the validity of the claim of this estate. I merely asked
you as the Honored head of this department, to see to it that this
claim is presented to the Japanese representatives here. My person-
al opinion 1s that the representatives of the Japanese Government
and i its fiscal agent, the said banking institution, know more
about this claim and ali the circumstances surrounding it, than any-
one inside of the case, but they prefer to indulge in a technical
court battle rather than give this estate the consideration that it
1s entitled to.
I have several times stressed the fact that the
anoillary administrator is a citizen of this country, so was the
decedent, and so is the sole legatee under the Will, and I say to
you, without mental reservation of any sort, that unless this Depart-
ment oomes to the aid of this estate, that there is a strong likel1-
hood that the ends of justioe will be defeated.
I repeat that I cannot, as the legal representative
of this estate, accept the position that you take in your letter of
July l7th, as final. Must this estate be the loser of a substantial
sum of money, because the other side blocks the avenue of litigation
with teohnical obstacles?
Under the oiroumstances, I must ask that the matter
be given further consideration, and that this claim be submitted to
the Imperial Japanese Government at the earltest possible moment.
With muoh respeot, I beg to remain,
HBC/L

SUBJECT:
Commercial Credits to.Japen.
MJNICAONS
AND RECORDS
PARTiciPANTs: Mr. F. J. Hellman, Vice President, Wells Fargo Bank
and Union Trust Compeny, San Frencisco,
Mr. Hamilton

5
Mr. Hellman stated that his company has been extend-
2
ing commercial credite to Japanese banks, principelly the
Bank of Chosen end the First Bank, in connection with
exports of California products to Japan, principelly rayon
FILED
pulp. He said that these credits are principally for
7
periods of ninety days, but latterly a number of them
3E
have been for six months and that they are eecured by
Japanese dollar bonds in the United States. Mr. Hellman
explained that, so far as he knew, other American banks
had discontinued granting such credits in l93l, following
F/WB
the Mukden incident, and that hie bank wes the only one
that was continuing in this practice; that as a result
of the policy of the bank it enjoyed considerable good
will among the Japanese, and Californian exporters were
enabled

=== Page 474 === (confidence 0.98)
134
-2-
enabled to do a volume of profitable business. Mr. Hellman
inquired whether, as a consequence of our notification of
desire to terminate the commercial treaty, there was any
reason why the bank should alter its polioy in respect to
these credits.
Mr. Hellman was reminded, in reply, of the action taken
by the Department of Commerce last year in advising American
exporters, in view of the exchange control policies adopted
by Japan which had resulted in freezing in Japan large
amounts of funds due to American firms, to restrict trans-
actions to those on a fully secured basis. He was told
that, while there was no law or regulation which prohibited
or restricted American banks in the matter of loans and
credits to Japan, the Department had, when approached on
this question by American businessmen,expressed the hope
that American business would be guided by consideration of
the effect upon Amerioan interests in general in the Far
East of the granting of such credits, having in mind Japanese
policies and actions, and looking at the question from a
long-range point of view. Mr. Hellman was informed that the
Department had not undertaken to define the range within
which short-term commercial credits might not be objectionable
but that it was our understanding that the general tendenoy
not only in this country but in certain other countries was
to shorten the term of any suoh commeroial oredits. It was
indicated

=== Page 475 === (confidence 0.93)
心
-3-
indioated to Mr. Hellman that this development was in
line with the attitude which the Department hoped would be
followed by American businessmen and bankers. It was also
pointed out to Mr. Hellman that this question was entirely
independent of the question of the forthcoming termination
of the commercial treaty with Japan of 19ll. Mr. Hellman
was asked to regard this expression of views on the question
of extending credits as confidential.

Supplementing the Embassy's despatch No. 118c,
dated January 22, 1931 (see also the mbassy's despatch
No. 1248, dated February 14, 1931), I have the honor to
transmit herewith, as of possible intarest to the Depart-
ment, the text of a letter addressed by the Council of
French Holdors of the City of Tokyo bonds to the Japanese
Ambassador in Paris, as well as comments on this question,
as clipped from L'I DU PEUPLE, dated February 13, 1931.
It will he observed the letter of the Council re-
calls, in referring to the recent favorable decision handed
dowm by the Supreme Court of Paris (reported in the Imbassy's
despatch No. 1l8o), that it was not within the province of
22320
838.51
that Jourt to settle certain points relative to payment, for
the

=== Page 534 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
the reason that the Court was oompetent in law but not in fact.
Recognizing that it is possible for the City of Tokyo to pro-
long legal procedure under these ciroumstanoes, the Council ap-
peals to the good faith of the City and expresses the hope that
it will inclino before the judgment of the Supreme Court and
not delay further a settlement "the principle of which has been
definitively judged".
It appears from the commeuts of L' LII DU PEUPLE which
follow this letter that the City of Tokyo is negotiating in
Paris a new loan of 3o0 million francs and that under these
circumstances the latter will perhaps consider it advi able
to accede to the request of the Council of Holders.
Respectfully yours,
Walter E. Edge

Paris, le 27 janvier 1931.
Monsieur l'Ambassadeur,
Après plusieurs années de procédure, la Cour de Cassation, notre plus haute juridiction, a
rendu son arrel le 14 janvier 1931, sur l'emprunt 5 0/0 1912 de la Ville de Tokio.
Par cct arrêt, clle a jugé d'une façon définitive guc la tranche française fait partie de l'em-
prunt unique de £ : 9.175.000, émis par la Ville de Tokio sur les différentes places de Londres,
New-York, Paris et Bruxelles, et qu'en conséquence, les porteurs français ont le droit, comme les
Anglais ct les Américains, d'être payés à la parité de la livre sterling.
C'était d'ailleurs l'opinion hautement impartiale émise dès 1926 par votre éminent juriscon-
sulte, M. le professeur Tokuso Fukuda.
Mais la Cour de Cassation ne pouvait pas, de par scs attributions spéciales, régler elle-même
certains points de détail relatifs au paiement, car elle juge cn droit seulement ct non en fait.
Par suite, il scrait encore possible à la Ville de Tokio d'éluder quelque temps l'exécution de
cet arrêt, en prolongeant la procédure, sans espoir d'aillcurs de faire modifier la décision acquise,
mais uniquement afin de retarder un règlement dont lc principe est définitivement jugé.

que municipalité, qu'attendre la décision de la justice, nous voulons cspérer qu'ellc tiendra mainte-
nant à honneur de s'incliner loyalement dcvant sa condamnation de principe émanant de notre
Cour Suprême, sans chercher à prolonger sa résistance pour gagner du temps.
Un tel acquiescement donné de bonne grâce serait hautement apprécić par l'opinion française
qui y verrait une marque éclatante de la loyauté de la Ville de Tokio cnvers les obligataires qui lui
ont fait confiance.
Nous nous permettons, en effet, d'attirer l'attention de votre Excellence sur la situation pénible
des portcurs irançais de l'emprunt 5 0/0 1912 qui, bien quc nc touchant plus leurs coupons depuis
plus de trois ans, ont dú faire face aux frais considérables des procès, tandis que la Ville de Tokio
bénéficiait, pendant ce temps, des intérêts importants des sommes qu'clle gardait en réserve en
attendant la décision judiciaire. C'est. pour le servicc de la tranche française, une somme de £: 240.000
par an que conserve la Ville de Tokio depuis fin 1927, el dont les portcurs sont privés chaque
annéc.
Nous faisons donc appel à votre haute bienvcillance pour vous prier d'insister auprès de la
Ville de Tokio afin qu'elle fasse connaltre, le plus rapidement possible, ses intentions, espérant

tente onéreuse des obligataires, dont le bon droit est reconnu.
Cet acquiescement ne peut que consolider l'estime et le crédit de la Vilie de Tokio auprès des
Français qui, confiants dans les traditions d'honneur et de loyauté dn peuple aponais, ont souscrit
à ses emprunts.

considération.

port that the terms of the agreement signed on Decem-
ber 20, 1939, between representatives of the French
11
FEB 7 - 1940
holders of the 5% 1912 loan of the City of Tokyo, and
FILED
representatives of the City, were published today.
The agreement comes into force on September 1,
1940. On that date the City of Tokyo will pay in
cash, in French francs, drawn bonds and matured
coupons of bonds redeemed, or of bonds unredeemed at
F/FG
that date, regardless of the date of redemption or of
maturity.
Furthermore,

=== Page 544 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
Furthermore, the City of Tokyo will deliver to
holders of all redeemed or unredeemed bonds, as well
as to holders of all coupons which matured before the
date (April 28, 1938) of the decision of the Court of
Appeal confirming the resolution of the general meeting
of holders of April 17, 1937, approving the transaction,
a security of a nominal value of 407.75 francs for one
bond or for 40 matured coupons.
The new securities will be redeemed in twenty-five
years and will carry an annual rate of interest of 5%.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
Maynard B. Barnes
Mrst Secretary of Embassy
In quintuplicate
